Coherent cochain complexes and Beilinson t-structures, with an appendix by Achim Krause
Stefano Ariotta

Abstract
. We define and study coherent cochain complexes in arbitrary stable ∞ \infty -categories, following Joyal. Our main result is that the ∞ \infty -category of coherent cochain complexes in a stable ∞ \infty -category C {C} is equivalent to the ∞ \infty -category of complete filtered objects in C {C} . We then show how the Beilinson t-structure can be interpreted in light of such equivalence, and analyze its behavior in the presence of symmetric monoidal structures. We also examine the relationship between the notion of (higher) Toda brackets and coherent cochain complexes. Finally, we prove how every coherent cochain complex gives rise to a spectral sequence and illustrate some examples.



1.
Introduction

Recall that a filtered chain complex (resp. a filtered spectrum) consists of a

\mathbb{Z}
-indexed sequence

\cdots\to F^{n}\to F^{n-1}\to F^{n-2}\to\cdots

where each
F^{i}
is a chain complex (resp. a spectrum) and the morphisms
between them are chain maps
1
1

1

in the case of chain
complexes it is common to allow only monomorphisms, but as one can always
replace a chain map by a monomorphic one up to quasi-isomorphism, this
extra condition will not be relevant to us

(resp. morphisms of spectra).

Two of the motivating reasons for the study of filtered derived categories
and filtered spectra are the construction of spectral sequences, and
the existence of the Beilinson t-structure (firstly
introduced in
[
Bei87
]
; see Definition
5.5
).
The objective of this paper is to present a new perspective on such objects
(and more generally, filtered objects in stable
\infty
-categories) that allows to
generalize at once both constructions in a homotopy coherent fashion,
and to gain some insight on other related constructions, like the obstruction
theory for the realizability of spectra with prescribed homotopy groups and
k-invariants (see Section
8
).
This perspective will be realized by using coherent cochain complexes
(originally introduced in their bounded flavor in
[
Joy08a
, 35.1]
,
in relation to the
\infty
-categorical Dold-Kan correspondence).

A coherent cochain complex in a stable
\infty
-category
{C}
is a homotopy coherent
version of an ordinary cochain complex, and consists of a
\mathbb{Z}
-indexed
sequence of objects
C^{i}\in{C}
and differentials

\cdots\xrightarrow{\partial}C^{n}\xrightarrow{\partial}C^{n+1}\xrightarrow{\partial}C^{n+2}\xrightarrow{\partial}\cdots

together with nullhomotopies
\partial^{2}\simeq 0
, and further
coherence data making all the nullhomotopies mutually compatible.
Concretely, a coherent cochain complex will be defined as a pointed functor
from (the nerve of) a
1
-category having as objects the integers together with
an extra base point, and being generated by morphisms
\partial\colon n\to n+1

such that
\partial^{2}=0
(see Definition
2.1
for a rigorous
formulation).
For the sake of simplicity, in this introduction we will restrict to the case
of spectra, and the corresponding
\infty
-category of coherent cochain complexes

\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})
, although the results in the rest of the paper are discussed in the
wider generality of stable
\infty
-categories equipped with some t-structure.

At their core, both the Beilinson t-structure and the spectral sequence
associated to a filtered spectrum are in a sense “blind” to the
information stored at the limit of the relevant filtered object.
As it is customary, we say that a filtered object
F
is
complete
if

\varprojlim F\simeq 0
, and we call the cofiber of the canonical map

\varprojlim F\to\varinjlim F
the
completion
of
\varinjlim F
.
If
F
is a filtered spectrum,
the spectral sequence it generates only abuts to the completion of its colimit.
Something similar is true for the Beilinson t-structure, although saying
precisely what this means requires more work;
the failure of it being left complete is a good starting point:
all and only the constant objects are the ones that are
\infty
-connected
with respect to the Beilinson t-structure, and constant objects are in a precise
sense orthogonal to complete ones (see Proposition

2.21
). In Theorem
5.11
, we prove that
the Beilinson t-structure can be glued out of its restriction to the full
subcategory of complete objects and the trivial t-structure on the full
subcategory of constant objects.

As it turns out, the
\infty
-category of filtered spectra is equivalent to that
of coherent cochain complexes of spectra:

Theorem 0

(see Theorems
3.19
,

4.7
and §
6
)
There exists a symmetric monoidal adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Sp})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}

\dashv

that restricts to an equivalence on complete filtered spectra.
Moreover, this adjunction sends the Day convolution symmetric monoidal
structure on filtered spectra, to a symmetric monoidal structure given
componentwise by

(C\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits D)^{n}\simeq\mathop{\oplus}\displaylimits_{s+t=n}C^{s}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits D^{t}.

As already noted in
[
Lur17
, 1.2]
, given a filtered spectrum
F
one can
construct a sequence of morphisms on suitable shifts of the graded pieces

(1)

\gr^{n}F[-n]\to\gr^{n+1}F[-n-1]

such that all pairwise compositions are nullhomotopic, and the homotopies are
suitably compatible (this is in fact the first step for the construction of
the spectral sequence of
F
).
The left adjoint defined in the theorem sends
F
to the coherent cochain
complex

\cdots\to\gr^{-2}F[-2]\to\gr^{-1}F[-1]\to\gr^{0}F\to\gr^{1}F[1]\to\gr^{2}F[2]\to\cdots

where the differentials are precisely the maps of (
1
).
Although above we only represented the components and the differentials of
the object
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}F
, it also encodes much more data:
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})
is
defined using a pointed diagram
1
-category and
pointed functors, hence its objects keep track also of the nullhomotopies
for the two-fold compositions of their differentials, and of all the
recursive coherences between them.
The compatibilities between all the nullhomotopies
can be made explicit by means of higher Toda brackets (see Section

7
); in order to do so, we use an explicit
\mathbb{E}_{1}
presentation
of exterior algebras over
\mathbb{Z}
due to Achim Krause, presented in
Appendix
B
.
The left adjoint above is really a homotopy coherent version
of the construction of homotopy objects in Beilinson’s t-structure: such
t-structure corresponds to the pointwise one along the left adjoint functor
discussed above; that is, for a filtered spectrum
F
,

\pi_{n}^{B}F\cong\pi_{n}^{\text{lvl}}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}F,

where
\pi_{n}^{\text{lvl}}C
denotes the functor applying

\pi_{n}
to all the components of
C
.

The right adjoint can be thought of as the functor
iteratively solving all the extension problems needed to reconstruct the
filtered object from its graded pieces and the differentials. Notice that,
in order for such a reconstruction to be possible in general, one really needs
to use all the information stored in the higher morphisms.
In fact, the differentials appearing in a coherent cochain complex encode
precisely the differentials of the
E_{1}
page of a spectral sequence
abutting to the
total homology
of the complex (that is, the object
underlying its associated filtered object). The higher pages together with
their differentials can be recovered
from the complex itself, by means of an incarnation of Deligne’s

décalage
construction, as explained in Section
9
.

1.1.
Outline of the paper

In Section
2
, we put the basis for the rest of the paper, by
recalling
the main definitions and some structural results about the
\infty
-categories at hand. In
particular, we show that for a stable
\infty
-category
{C}
,
both
\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
and
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
sit in suitable recollements of
stable
\infty
-categories.

Section
3
is the technical core of the paper, in which we
construct the adjunction between filtered spectra
and cochain complexes of spectra, proving the equivalence of the latter
with the
\infty
-category of complete filtered spectra. Then, in Section

4
, we
generalize the result to all stable
\infty
-categories with sequential (inverse) limits.

In Section
5
, we analyze the interplay between the
Beilinson t-structure and the levelwise t-structure on the relevant categories,
when the base
\infty
-category
{C}
comes equipped with a t-structure. We continue the
analysis of
\infty
-categories with extra structure in Section
6
, where
we study the interplay of the equivalence of Theorem

4.7
with symmetric monoidal structures, and the
compatibility of the relevant t-structures in the case where the base
{C}

is also equipped with a t-structure.

In Section
7
, we use an explicit
\mathbb{E}_{1}
presentation
of exterior algebras over
\mathbb{Z}
, proven by Achim Krause in
Appendix
B
, to show that
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
is the universal
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}
-indexed sequences of morphisms in
{C}
such that any pairwise
composition is trivial, as are all the possible Toda brackets.

In Section
8
, we show how coherent cochain complexes encode
a form of obstruction theory, and we use the formalism developed there to
recover a spectral sequence from a coherent cochain complex in
Section
9
.

Finally, in Section
10
, we have a look at a few examples
from the recent literature that we believe benefit from the perspective of
coherent cochain complexes.

Some of the main tools for constructing the adjunction and proving
the equivalence of Theorem
4.7
is
the stable nerve-realization paradigm. We
recollect and prove many results about the
\infty
-categorical incarnation
of this topic in Appendix
A
.

1.2.
Notational conventions

Throughout, we will use the following notations and terminology:

(1)

We will denote by

\mathcal{P}({C})\coloneqq\Fun({C}^{\mathrm{op}},{S})

the
\infty
-category of presheaves on
{C}
.

(2)

We will denote by

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C})\coloneqq\Fun({C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Sp})

the
\infty
-category of spectra-valued presheaves on
{C}
, and sometimes refer to
it as the
\infty
-category of
stable presheaves
on
{C}
.

(3)

We will denote by

\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\colon{C}\to\mathcal{P}({C})

the Yoneda embedding, and by
\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{\kern-1.63885ptC}
the functor represented by an
object
C\in{C}
.

(4)

Given any functor
\infty
-category
\Fun({C},{D})
, we will denote by

\textsc{cns}\colon{D}\to\Fun({C},{D})

the (fully faithful) functor obtained by precomposition with the terminal
functor
{C}\to\Delta^{0}
, and by
\textsc{cns}_{C}
its value at
C\in{C}
.

(5)

When considering
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{N}
as categories, we will always
implicitly assume that they are given the structure of a poset category,
with their usual ordering
\leqslant
.
We will use the notations
\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}
and

\mathbb{N}^{\delta}
to denote
their underlying discrete categories.

(6)

Unless otherwise stated, whenever we refer to
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}
or

\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}
as symmetric monoidal categories, we consider them endowed
with the symmetric monoidal structure induced by addition.

(7)

We refer to a presentable
\infty
-category endowed with a symmetric monoidal
structure whose tensor product preserves colimits in each variable as a

presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
.

(8)

We refer to a stable
\infty
-category endowed with a symmetric monoidal
structure whose tensor product is exact in each variable as a

stably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
.

(9)

We refer to a presentable stable
\infty
-category endowed with a symmetric monoidal
structure whose tensor product preserves colimits in each variable as a

stable presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
.

(10)

We will most of the times omit the Eilenberg–MacLane functor from
the notations, when considering an Abelian group as a spectrum.

Remark 1.1

Throughout, when dealing with t-structures, we will use the homological
grading convention.
When dealing with spectral sequences, we will use the cohomological
Serre grading convention.
For the sake of clarity, we decided to stick with
the choice of working exclusively with decreasing filtered objects and
coherent cochain complexes.
The results of this paper translate immediately
to the case of increasing filtrations and coherent chain complexes,
with the caveat that the equivalence of Theorem
4.7

always inverts
the direction of the
\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}
-indexed arrows, hence complete

increasing
filtered objects are equivalent to coherent
chain

complexes.

Remark 1.2

Throughout, we (mostly implicitly)
work in ZFC+U, where “U” is Tarski’s axiom: “For each set
x
, there
exists a Grothendieck universe
U
such that
x\in U
”.
Equivalently we assume the existence of infinitely many strongly inaccessible
cardinals, and we fix one as the cardinality of our universe of small sets.
As we are agnostic about the size of the universe fixed, all the arguments
that do not rely on dealing with a bigger universe hold regardless of
the actual size of the objects referred to as “small sets”.
In those few cases where we need to deal with more than one universe at a
time, we are going to be explicit about relative sizes.

1.3.
Related works

Some of the structural results about filtered objects in stable
\infty
-categories contained in Section
3
have been presented for the
case of filtered spectra in
[
Lur15
, Sections 2-3]
.
The
\infty
-category of filtered objects of a stable
\infty
-category has also been studied in

[
GP18
, Section 2]
, and some results in Section

3
and Section
6
overlap with

loc. cit.
2
2

2

the reader should note that in

[
GP18
]
the authors refer to what we call

filtered
objects as “sequences”, and reserve the term “filtered
objects” for what we call
complete filtered objects
.
In
[
Rak20
]
, Raksit considers an alternative formulation for coherent
chain complexes; although we don’t dwelve into a detailed comparison,
the results of Section
7
, should give a clear idea about
the relation
between the formulation in
op. cit.
and the one in this paper.
In
[
Wal19
]
, Walde proves the equivalence between
\Fun(\mathbb{N},{C})
and

\operatorname{Ch}_{*\geqslant 0}({C})
through their equivalence with

\Fun(\reflectbox{
\scalebox{1.5}[1.1]{\text{$\mathbbm{\Delta}$}}}\kern-5.0pt^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
.
A similar approach to the construction of the spectral sequence of a filtered
spectrum through the décalage functor
that does not use the language of coherent cochain complexes appeared
recently in Hedenlund’s Ph.D. thesis
[
Hed21
]
.
The décalage functor introduced in Section
9
is
further analyzed and discussed in forthcoming work by
Hedenlund–Krause–Nikolaus.

1.4.
Prerequisites

We assume the reader is familiar with the theory of
\infty
-categories as developed
in
[
Lur09
]
and with the contents of
[
Lur17
]
. In particular, we assume
the reader is thoroughly acquainted with the theory of t-structures as
developed in
[
Lur17
, 1.2.1]
(see also
[
AN20
, Appendix A]
).
Some of the terminology and notations in this paper differ from the ones used
in
[
Lur09
]
and
[
Lur17
]
, but we use the same notations and terminology
of
op. cit.
for the concepts we do not explicitly recall or introduce
here.

article.

2.
Definitions and preliminaries

In this section we introduce and discuss a few key facts about coherent
cochain complexes and filtered objects in
\infty
-categories.
In particular, in Proposition
2.20
and Proposition

2.21
we prove that both constructions figure in suitable
recollements (see Definition
2.13
) of stable
\infty
-categories.

Definition 2.1

[
Joy08a
, 35.1]

Let
\mathbf{Ch}
be the pointed (ordinary) category given by

\operatorname{ob}\mathbf{Ch}=\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\mathrm{pt}\}

\mathbf{Ch}(n,m)=\begin{cases}\{\mathrm{id},\mathbf{0}\}\quad&\text{if }m=n;\\
\{\partial_{n},\mathbf{0}\}\quad&\text{if }m=n-1;\\
\{\mathbf{0}\}\quad&\text{otherwise }.\end{cases}

where
\mathrm{pt}\in\mathbf{Ch}
is a zero object, and
\mathbf{0}
is the zero map.

Given any pointed
\infty
-category
{C}

(1)

the
\infty
-category of
coherent chain complexes in
{C}
is the full
subcategory

\operatorname{Ch}_{*}({C})\coloneqq\Fun^{0}(\mathbf{Ch},{C})\subset\Fun(\mathbf{Ch},{C})

spanned by pointed functors.

(2)

The
\infty
-category of
coherent cochain complexes
is the full
subcategory

\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})\coloneqq\Fun^{0}(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})\subset\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})

spanned by pointed functors.

(3)

An object
C\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
is
bounded above
if
there exists an
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

C^{k}\simeq 0\text{ for all }k>n.

(4)

An object
C\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
is
bounded below
if
there exists an
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

C^{k}\simeq 0\text{ for all }k<n.

(5)

An object
C\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
is
bounded
if it is
bounded above and bounded below.

Remark 0

Notice that
0\in\mathbb{Z}
is an object of
\mathbf{Ch}
, but it is not its zero
object.

Example 2.2

Let
{A}
be an Abelian category, then
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({A})
is the usual category
of cochain complexes of
{A}
.

In what follows, we will exclusively focus our attention on coherent cochain
complexes.

Notation 2.3

Given a coherent cochain complex
C\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{{C}}
, we will denote
C(\partial_{n})

by
\partial^{n}_{C}
, or just by
\partial^{n}
if there is no risk of confusion.

Definition 2.4

Let
{C}
be any
\infty
-category.

(1)

The category
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}\coloneqq\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
is called
the
\infty
-category of
(decreasing) filtered objects of
{C}
.

(2)

The category
\mathrm{Fil}_{\text{\raisebox{1.3pt}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\uparrow$}}}}{C}\coloneqq\Fun(\mathbb{Z},{C})
is called the

\infty
-category of
(increasing) filtered objects of
{C}

3
3

3

the position of the arrows in the notation is meant to remind
the common convention of using upper indices for decreasing filtrations,
and lower indices for increasing filtrations
.

(3)

Given
F\in\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
, we call
F^{-\infty}\coloneqq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{i}F^{i}

the
underlying object of
F
.

(4)

Given
F\in\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
, we say that
F
is
complete
if

F^{+\infty}\coloneqq\lim_{i}F^{i}\simeq 0
.
We will denote by

\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}

the full subcategory
of
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
spanned by complete objects.

Throughout, we will concentrate on the case of descending filtrations.

Definition 2.5

Let
{C}
be an
\infty
-category with cofibers and countable coproducts.
Precomposition with the inclusion map
\iota_{n}\colon\Delta^{\{n+1,n\}}\to\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}

induces functors

\begin{split}(\iota_{n})^{*}\colon\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}&\to\Fun(\Delta^{1},{C})\\
F&\mapsto\left(F^{n+1}\to F^{n}\right).\end{split}

(1)

We will define the
n
-th graded functor

\gr^{n}
as the
composite functor

\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}\xrightarrow{(\iota_{n})^{*}}\Fun(\Delta^{1},{C})\xrightarrow{\cofib}{C}.

(2)

We define the
associated graded functor

\gr
as the
composite functor

\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}\xrightarrow{\left(\gr^{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{C}\xrightarrow{\bigoplus}{C}.

Definition 2.6

We say that a map
\alpha\colon F\to G
in
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
is a
graded
equivalence
if
\alpha\colon F\to G
is such that
\gr(\alpha)
is an
equivalence. Equivalently. if for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the dotted map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr^{n}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr^{n}G}

induced by universality of cofibers is an equivalence.

Notation 2.7

Let
{C}
be an
\infty
-category. For
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\cup\{+\infty,-\infty\}
, with

n\leqslant m
, we use the notation

F^{n}/F^{m}\coloneqq\cofib\left(F^{m}\to F^{n}\right)

to denote the cofiber of the evident map.

One of the nice features of ordinary cochain complexes is the possibility to
write them as limits of bounded above (or below) ones. This feature is still
present in the coherent setting, as we now show.

Construction 2.8

Given any integer
n
, let
\mathbf{Ch}_{(-\infty,n]}

denote the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Ch}
spanned by

\{\mathrm{pt},n,n-1,n-2,\cdots\}
.
Let now
{C}
denote a pointed complete
4
4

4

or, more generally, such
that all the relevant Kan extensions exist and are pointwise

\infty
-category. Then, each inclusion
\iota^{n}\colon\mathbf{Ch}_{(-\infty,n]}\to\mathbf{Ch}

induces, by right Kan extension along it, an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun^{0}\left(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}_{(-\infty,n]},{C}\right).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{n})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n}_{*}}

\dashv

For any given
C\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
, we can compute explicitly the values of

\iota^{n}_{*}C
.
As
\iota^{n}
is fully faithful, the only values we need to determine are
the ones for
M>n
:

\begin{split}\iota^{n}_{*}C(M)\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{rem-end-ran-formula})}}}{{\simeq}}&\int_{s\in\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}_{(-\infty,n]}}\left[\Map_{\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}}(M,s),C^{s}\right]\\
\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{\hphantom{(\ref{rem-end-ran-formula})}}}}{{\simeq}}&\int_{s\in\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}_{(-\infty,n]}}[\mathrm{pt},C^{s}]\\
\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{cor-dummy-coend})}}}{{\simeq}}&\lim_{\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}_{(-\infty,n]}}C\\
\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{\hphantom{(\ref{cor-dummy-coend})}}}}{{\simeq}}&0\end{split}

where the last limit is
0
as the indexing category is pointed, and
C

preserves the zero object.
If we now denote by
(-)^{\leqslant n}\coloneqq\iota^{n}_{*}(\iota^{n})^{*}
, we have
that the unit of the adjunction
(\iota^{n})^{*}\dashv\iota^{n}_{*}
induces an
endofunctor of
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
, denoted
(-)^{\leqslant n}
,
sending a complex
C
to the complex
C^{\leqslant n}
, which in degree
m

is given by

\begin{cases}C^{m}&\text{ if }m\leqslant n\\
0&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Similarly, the inclusions
\iota^{n,n+1}\colon\mathbf{Ch}_{(-\infty,n]}\to\mathbf{Ch}_{(-\infty,n+1]}
induce adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun^{0}\left(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}_{(-\infty,n+1]},{C}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun^{0}\left(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}_{(-\infty,n]},{C}\right).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota^{n,n+1})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n,n+1}_{*}}

\dashv

Notice that we have a natural equivalence

(\iota^{n+1}\circ\iota^{n,n+1})^{*}\simeq(\iota^{n,n+1})^{*}(\iota^{n+1})^{*}\simeq(\iota^{n})^{*};

by passing to adjoints, we get a natural transformation

(2)

(\iota^{n+1})^{*}\Rightarrow\iota^{n,n+1}_{*}(\iota^{n})^{*};

if we now precompose (
2
)
with
\iota^{n+1}_{*}
(using that adjunctions compose), we get a natural
transformation

\upsilon^{n+1}\colon(-)^{\leqslant n+1}\Rightarrow(-)^{\leqslant n}.

By inspection, the above is given pointwise by

(\upsilon^{n+1}_{C})^{m}\simeq\begin{cases}\mathrm{id}\colon C^{m}\to C^{m}&\text{ if }m\leqslant n\\
0\colon C^{n+1}\to 0&\text{ if }m=n+1\\
0\colon 0\to 0&\text{ if }m\geqslant n+2.\end{cases}

Remark 2.9

Similarly to what we did in Construction
2.8
, one can
truncate
below
a certan integer. It is also possible to consider

left
Kan extensions along the
\iota^{n}
’s, and the induced counits
to obtain different truncations with
\cofib\partial^{n}
in degree
n+1
,
for truncations above
n
, or
\fib\partial^{n}
in degree
n-1
,
for truncations below
n
. In what follows, we won’t need any of such
variants.

Lemma 2.10

Let
{C}
be a pointed complete
\infty
-category. Then, for any
C\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}

C\simeq\lim\left(\cdots\to C^{\leqslant n+1}\xrightarrow{\upsilon^{n+1}_{C}}C^{\leqslant n}\to\cdots\right)

(where the
\upsilon^{n}
’s are the natural transformations defined in
Construction
2.8
).

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
2.20
that limits in

\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
can be computed objectwise. As for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
the
sequence

\cdots\to\left(C^{\leqslant n+1}\right)^{m}\xrightarrow{\upsilon^{n}_{C}}\left(C^{\leqslant n}\right)^{m}\to\cdots

is eventually constant on the left, the result follows.
∎

Lemma 2.11

Given any pointed
\infty
-category
{C}
,
the functor
u\colon\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})\to\prod_{\mathbb{Z}}{C}
induced by
precomposition with
\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}\to\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}
is conservative.

Proof.

As equivalences in
\infty
-categories are detected at the level of homotopy categories,
this is clear.
∎

Proposition 2.12

Let
{C}
be a complete and cocomplete semiadditive
\infty
-category, and let

n^{*}\colon\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})\to\Fun(\Delta^{0},{C})

denote precomposition with
n\colon\Delta^{0}\to\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
The “evaluation at
n
” functor

\mathrm{ev}_{n}\colon\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})\subset\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})\xrightarrow{n^{*}}{C},

admits both a left and a right adjoint.
Such adjoints are given objectwise by

(\mathrm{ev}_{n})_{*}X^{m}\simeq\begin{cases}X\quad&\text{if }m=n-1,n\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

with the identity as the only nontrivial differential, and

(\mathrm{ev}_{n})_{!}X^{m}\simeq\begin{cases}X\quad&\text{if }m=n,n+1\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

with the identity as the only nontrivial differential.

Proof.

The functor
n^{*}
admits both adjoints
n_{!}
and
n_{*}
, given respectively
by left and right Kan extension.
As adjoint functors compose,
it follows from Proposition
2.20
that the right
Kan extension
(\mathrm{ev}_{n})_{*}
is given by the composite
\textsc{pt}\circ n_{*}
,
and the left Kan extension
(\mathrm{ev}_{n})_{!}
is given by
\textsc{pt}\circ n_{!}
.
By Remark
A.12
,
n_{*}
is given by

n_{*}X^{m}=[\Map_{\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}}(m,n),X]=\begin{cases}X\oplus X\quad&\text{if }m=n-1,n\\
X&\text{else.}\end{cases}

with the differentials
n_{*}X^{m}\to n_{*}X^{m+1}
determined by

\Map_{\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}}(m+1,n)\xrightarrow{(\partial_{m}^{\mathrm{op}})^{*}}\Map_{\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}}(m,n)

and thus given by

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{id}\\
\mathrm{id}\end{pmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{id}&0\\
0&\mathrm{id}\end{pmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{id}&0\end{pmatrix}}

in degrees
n-2
to
n+1
, and by identities elsewhere.

By Lemma
A.11
,
n_{!}
is given by

n_{*}X^{m}=\Map_{\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}}(n,m)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits X=\begin{cases}X\oplus X\quad&\text{if }m=n,n+1\\
X&\text{else.}\end{cases}

with the differentials
n_{*}X^{m}\to n_{*}X^{m+1}
determined by

\Map_{\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}}(n,m)\xrightarrow{(\partial_{m}^{\mathrm{op}})_{*}}\Map_{\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}}(n,m+1)

and thus given by

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{id}\\
0\end{pmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{id}&0\\
0&\mathrm{id}\end{pmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{id}&\mathrm{id}\end{pmatrix}}

in degrees
n-1
to
n+2
, and by identities elsewhere.

By the explicit description of
pt
given in Proposition

2.20
, we get the formulas for
(\mathrm{ev}_{n})_{*}
and

(\mathrm{ev}_{n})_{!}
.
∎

We now recall some definitions and facts about recollements,
which we will use extensively in the following sections; we consider only
recollements in the case of stable
\infty
-categories,
but the theory holds in greater generality; see also

[
FL15
]
,
[
BG16
]
and
[
Lur17
, A.8]
.

Definition 2.13

Let
{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category, and let
i\colon{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow{C}
and

j\colon{C}_{1}\hookrightarrow{C}
be full subcategories.
We say that

{C}
is a
recollement of the essential image of
i
and
the essential image of
j
if:

(1)

Both
i
and
j
admit left adjoints:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

\dashv

\dashv

(2)

The functor
j_{L}
, left adjoint to
j
, carries every object of

{C}_{0}
to zero;

(3)

If
\alpha
is a morphism of
{C}
such that
i_{L}(\alpha)
and

j_{L}(\alpha)
are equivalences, then
\alpha
is an equivalence.

Remark 2.14

It follows from
[
Lur17
, A.8.5, A.8.19]
that if
{C}
is a recollement of

{C}_{0}
and
{C}_{1}
, then we actually have the following adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(j_{L})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{R}}

\dashv

\dashv

\dashv

\dashv

where
(j_{L})_{!}
is fully faithful, and
i_{R}
is such that

ii_{R}\to\mathrm{id}_{C}\to jj_{L}

is a co/fiber sequence.

Proposition 2.15

[
Lur17
, Proposition A.8.20]

Let
{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category, and let
i\colon{C}_{0}\to{C}
be a fully
faithful functor. The following are equivalent:

(1)

The functor
i
admits a left adjoint and a right adjoint;

(2)

There exists a full subcategory
j\colon{C}_{1}\hookrightarrow{C}
,
closed under equivalences, such that
{C}
is the recollement of the
essential images of
i
and
j
.

Moreover, if the conditions above hold, we can identify
{C}_{1}
with
the full subcategory
{C}_{0}^{\perp}\subseteq{C}
spanned by those
objects
X\in{C}
such that for all
C\in{C}_{0}
,

\Map_{{C}}(C,X)\simeq\mathrm{pt}.

Recollements are strictly related to semiorthogonal decompositions.

Definition 2.16

Let
{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category. A
semiorthogonal decomposition
of

{C}
is the datum of two full subcategories
{C}_{0}
and
{C}_{1}
of

{C}
such that

(1)

{C}_{1}\simeq{C}_{0}^{\perp}
;

(2)

Every object
C\in{C}
sits in a cofiber sequence

C_{0}\to C\to C_{1}

where
C_{0}\in{C}_{0}
and
C_{1}\in{C}_{1}
.

Remark 2.17

It follows from Remark
2.14
that every recollement
determines a semiorthogonal decomposition.

We learned the following argument from
[
BG16
, Proof of Lemma 3]
, and
we present it here almost verbatim for the reader’s convenience.

Proposition 2.18

The commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ii_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces jj_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ii_{L}jj_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ii_{L}\eta^{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{j}jj_{L}}

is Cartesian in
\Fun({C},{C})
.

Proof.

It follows from Remark
2.14
that the fiber of the
vertical maps is given by
\eta^{i}ii_{R}\colon ii_{R}\to ii_{L}ii_{R}
,
which, as
ii_{L}\simeq\mathrm{id}
, is an equivalence.
∎

Remark 2.19

In particular, every recollement determines a “fracture square”

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{L}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{L}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{L}j_{L}C}

for any object
C\in{C}
.

We are now ready to prove the main results of this section.

Proposition 2.20

Let
{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category that is both complete and cocomplete.
The
\infty
-category
\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
is the recollement of the essential images
of
\textsc{cns}\colon{C}\to\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
and
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})}
cns
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
pt
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim}

\dashv

\dashv

\dashv

\dashv

Moreover, the inclusion
i\colon\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})\to\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
is both
left and right adjoint to a functor
pt
, given by

\textsc{pt}F(n)\simeq F(n)/F(\mathrm{pt})

on objects.
Finally, the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
is stable, complete and cocomplete;
if
{C}
is presentable,
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
is presentable as well.

Proof.

The functor
cns
admits a left and a right adjoint, given respectively
by left and right Kan extension along the terminal morphism (i.e. the

\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits
and
\lim
functors). Hence, by Proposition

2.15
,
\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})

is the recollement of (the essential image of)
cns
and the full
subcategory
\textsc{cns}({C})^{\perp}
orthogonal to it.
We claim that

(5)

\textsc{cns}({C})^{\perp}\simeq\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C}).

To see this, recall that
\textsc{cns}({C})^{\perp}
is spanned by those objects

F\in\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
such that for all
X\in{C}
the mapping space

\Map_{\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})}(\textsc{cns}_{X},F)
is contractible. But, as

\Map_{\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})}(\textsc{cns}_{X},F)\simeq\Map_{{C}}(X,F(\mathrm{pt})),

we see that (
5
) holds.
We denote by
pt
the left adjoint to the fully faithful inclusion

i\colon\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})\to\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C}).

Now,
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})

is stable by
[
Lur17
, A.8.17]
(or just because of the fact that (co)limits
in functor categories are computed pointwise)
and, if
{C}
is presentable (as
\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
is presentable
by
[
Lur09
, 5.5.3.6]
)
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
is presentable by

[
Lur17
, 1.4.4.9]
. By
[
Lur17
, A.8.5]
, we see that

(6)

\textsc{pt}F\simeq\cofib\left(\textsc{cns}_{F(\mathrm{pt})}\to F\right)

and in particular
\textsc{pt}F(n)\simeq F(n)/F(\mathrm{pt})
.
The existence of a left adjoint for
pt

follows from Remark
2.14
.
To give a description, let’s first note that, by (
6
)
and the fully faithfulness of the inclusion
i

we can write

\begin{split}\Map_{\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})}(C,\textsc{pt}X)&\simeq\Map_{\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})}\big(C,\cofib(\textsc{cns}_{F(\mathrm{pt})}\to X)\big)\\
&\simeq\Map_{\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})}\big(C[-1],\fib(\textsc{cns}_{F(\mathrm{pt})}\to X)\big)\end{split}

and, as corepresentable functors commute with limits, the latter is
equivalent to

\fib\left(\Map_{\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})}\left(C[-1],\textsc{cns}_{F(\mathrm{pt})}\right)\to\Map_{\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})}\left(C[-1],X\right)\right)

which in turn, as
cns
is right adjoint to
\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits
, can be computed as

(7)

\fib\left(\Map_{{C}}\big(\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits C[-1],F(\mathrm{pt})\big)\to\Map_{\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})}\left(C[-1],X\right)\right).

But, as the colimit of a coherent cochain complex is always
0
, we have
that

\Map_{{C}}\big(\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits C[-1],F(\mathrm{pt})\big)\simeq\mathrm{pt}

and thus (
7
) is equivalent to

\begin{split}\fib\left(\mathrm{pt}\to\Map_{\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})}\left(C[-1],X\right)\right)\simeq&\ \Omega\Map_{\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})}\left(C[-1],X\right)\\
\simeq&\ \Map_{\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})}\left(C,X\right).\end{split}

In particular, as
pt
is both left and right adjoint to the inclusion,
we have that
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
is closed under all limits and colimits
in
\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
, which is by hypothesis complete and cocomplete.
∎

Proposition 2.21

Let
{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category that is both complete and cocomplete.
The
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
is the recollement of the essential images
of
\textsc{cns}\colon{C}\to\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
and
i\colon\hbox{\hskip }$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hskip 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-188.3508pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{40911075}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern }$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}.}
cns
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim}

\dashv

\dashv

\dashv

\dashv

The left adjoint to the inclusion
i

is given by Bousfield localization at the class of graded equivalences,
and computed as

LF^{n}\simeq F^{n}/F^{+\infty}.

Moreover, the
\infty
-category
\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}
is stable, complete and cocomplete; if

{C}
is presentable,
\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}
is presentable as well.

Proof.

The proof is completely analogous to the proof of
Proposition
2.20
. The only new element is the
identification of the local maps for the Bousfield localization determined
by
L
. In order to prove it,
let us notice that
L\alpha
is an equivalence if and only
if
\cofib L\alpha\simeq L(\cofib\alpha)\simeq 0
, which in turn,
by
[
Lur17
, A.8.5]
, is the case if and only if
\cofib\alpha
is
essentially constant;
by inspection of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr^{n}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr^{n}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\cofib\alpha)^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\cofib\alpha)^{n}}

where all the rows and columns are co/fiber sequences,
we see that this is the case if and only if
\alpha
is a graded equivalence.
∎

We conclude this section with the following fact about fully faithful
adjoint functors, which we will use later.

Proposition 2.22

Let
{C}
and
{D}
be
\infty
-categories, and let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

\dashv

be an adjunction. If there exists a natural isomorphism
\alpha\colon\mathrm{id}_{{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Longrightarrow}}GF
then the unit of the
adjunction is an equivalence (equivalently,
F
is fully faithful).

Proof.

[
RV16
]
, it is possible to associate to any adjunction a homotopy
coherent monad on
GF
, whose 1-skeletal part looks as follows

Now, by unitality,
\mu\widetilde{\eta}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{id}_{{C}}}
,
and as the following diagram commutes by naturality of
\mu
(or of

\widetilde{\eta}
),

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\eta}}

we see that
\widetilde{\eta}\mu\simeq\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{id}_{{C}}}
. Thus,
\widetilde{\eta}
is an
equivalence, and so is
\eta
.
∎

3.
Filtered spectra and cochain complexes of spectra

Our goal in this section is to construct an equivalence between the
\infty
-categories
\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}\mathrm{Sp}
and
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})
. In order to do so, we will first
construct a pair of adjoint functors between
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}\mathrm{Sp}
and
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})
using
the machinery of Appendix
A
, and then prove that the right
adjoint is a fully faithful functor having
\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}\mathrm{Sp}
as its essential image.
Along the way, we compute explicitly the values of the pair of adjoints
constructed abstractly.

In order to construct the left adjoint, let us start with the following
observation.

Lemma 3.1

Restriction along
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}(\mathbb{Z})
induces an equivalence
of
\infty
-categories

\Fun^{L}(\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}(\mathrm{Sp}),\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp}))\simeq\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp}))

whose inverse is given by
associating to every functor
F\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})
its stable
realization functor (see Definition
A.17
).

Proof.

As, by Proposition
2.20
,
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})
is a stable

\infty
-category, this is just an application of Lemma
A.18
, toghether
with the observation that
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}\mathrm{Sp}\simeq\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}(\mathbb{Z})
.
∎

According to the previous lemma, we will have to provide a functor

\mathbb{Z}\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})
in order to get the wanted left adjoint. A priori,
constructing such a functor would require to keep track of an infinite
amount of coherences, but it turns out that such coherences are essentially
trivial.
This idea is made precise by the following results.
The content of Proposition
3.3
is a corollary of

[
Lur19
, Tag 00J6]
; we report a direct proof here for the reader’s
convenience.

Notation 3.2

For any
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathrm{I}_{[m,n]}
denote the simplicial set

\Delta^{\{m,m-1\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{m-1\}}}\cdots\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{n-2\}}}\Delta^{\{n-2,n-1\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{n-1\}}}\Delta^{\{n-1,n\}}

which can be informally represented as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n-1}

Similarly, we denote by
\mathrm{I}_{[-\infty,n]}
and

\mathrm{I}_{[n,+\infty]}
respectively, the simplicial sets

\cdots\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{n-2\}}}\Delta^{\{n-2,n-1\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{n-1\}}}\Delta^{\{n-1,n\}},\quad\Delta^{\{n,n+1\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{n+1}}\Delta^{\{n+1,n+2\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta{n+2}}\cdots

and finally by

\mathrm{I}_{[-\infty,+\infty]}\coloneqq\cdots\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{n-1\}}}\Delta^{\{n-1,n\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{n\}}}\Delta^{\{n,n+1\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{n+1\}}}\cdots

which can be depicted as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n-1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n+1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

Proposition 3.3

The inclusion

\mathrm{I}_{[-\infty,+\infty]}\to\mathbb{Z}

is inner anodyne.

Proof.

Let us begin observing that, as shown in the proof of

[
Joy08b
, Proposition 2.13]
, for any
s>0
, both

\Delta^{\{0,1\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{1\}}}\Delta^{\{1,\ldots,s\}}\to\Delta^{s}

and

\Delta^{s-1}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{s-1\}}}\Delta^{\{s-1,s\}}\to\Delta^{s}

are inner anodyne.
By virtue of the following commutative diagram of simplicial sets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\{0,1\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{1\}}}\Delta^{\{1,\ldots,s\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\{0,1\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{1\}}}\Delta^{\{1,\ldots,s\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{s\}}}\Delta^{\{s,s+1\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{s}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{s\}}}\Delta^{\{s,s+1\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{s+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

where the square is a pushout, since the left vertical map is inner anodyne,
the right vertical map is inner anodyne, and, as the right horizontal
map is inner anodyne, the composite
\alpha
is inner anodyne as well.

Now, for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
P_{m}
denote the following pushout of
simplicial sets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{I}_{[-m,m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\{-m,\ldots,m\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{I}_{[-\infty,\infty]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{m}.}

As, by what we have seen above, the left vertical map in the following
pushout square of simplicial sets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\{0,1\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{1\}}}\Delta^{\{1,\ldots,s\}}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{\Delta^{\{s\}}}\Delta^{\{s,s+1\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{\{-m,\ldots,m\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{m+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{m}}

is inner anodyne, we have that
\beta_{m}\colon P_{m}\to P_{m+1}
is inner
anodyne for any
m>0
.

Finally, as the colimit projection
\gamma
below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{I}_{[-\infty,+\infty]}\simeq P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{m}P_{m}\simeq\mathbb{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}

is a transfinite composition of inner anodyne maps, it is inner anodyne.
∎

Corollary 3.4

Let
{C}
be an
\infty
-category. To specify a functor
\mathbb{Z}\to{C}
it is sufficient
to specify its value on objects and on morphisms of the form
n\to n+1
for
all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Construction 3.5

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})
be the functor defined on objects as

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(n)=\mathbb{S}^{n}_{[n]}
, where

\begin{split}\mathbb{S}^{n}_{[n]}\colon\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}&\to\mathrm{Sp}\\
m&\mapsto\begin{cases}\mathbb{S}[n]\quad&\text{if }m=n,\\
0\quad&\text{if }m\neq n.\end{cases}\end{split}

We define
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
on morphisms as follows.
First of all, let us note that, as

\begin{split}\Fun(\Delta^{1},\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp}))&\simeq\Fun(\Delta^{1},\Fun^{0}(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Sp}))\\
&\subset\Fun(\Delta^{1},\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Sp}))\\
&\simeq\Fun(\Delta^{1}\times\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Sp})\end{split}

it is equivalent to determine a map in
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})
or a map from
\Delta^{1}\times\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}
landing in spectra, such that both restrictions to

\{0\}\times\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\{1\}\times\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}
preserve zero objects
(recall that
\Fun^{0}(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Sp})
is a full subcategory of
\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathrm{Sp})
).
Let
\iota_{m}\colon\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}_{[m,m+1]}\to\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}
be the inclusion functor
Then, we define
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(\iota_{m})
as the morphism
corresponding to the following left Kan extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}\times\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}_{[m,m+1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}\times\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\times\iota_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

where
\alpha
is obtained from the defining square for the suspension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[m]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[m+1]}

and extending this map
\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}\to\mathrm{Sp}
to

\Delta^{1}\times\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}}
in the only possible way that sends
(0,\mathrm{pt})

and
(1,\mathrm{pt})
to the zero spectrum.
As
\mathrm{id}\times d_{m}
is fully faithful, the values of
\beta(0,\mathrm{pt})
and

\beta(1,\mathrm{pt})
are determined by
\alpha
, and are zero by construction.
By Corollary
3.4
, this completes the construction of

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
.

Definition 3.6

Let
5
5

5

where
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
stands for “Aushülen”, German for
hull
shelling

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\coloneqq\lvert-\rvert_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}^{\rm{st}}\colon\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}(\mathrm{Sp})\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})
denote the stable

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
-realization
functor associated, by the equivalence of Lemma
3.1
,
to the functor
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
given in
Construction
3.5
.
Let us denote by
6
6

6

where
\mathcal{I}
stands for “Impilare”, Italian
for
piling up

\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\nerve_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}^{\rm{st}}
its right
adjoint

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}\mathrm{Sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}

\dashv

(the reason for the hat in the notation will be clearified in Proposition

3.9
).
We will refer to
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
as the
shelling functor
functor
and to the
\mathcal{I}
as the
piling-up functor
; we will use the terms

associated shelled complex
and
piled-up filtered object
for
objects of the form
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}F
and
\mathcal{I}C
, respectively.

Remark 3.7

It follows from Proposition
A.23
that, for any coherent
cochain complex
C
, the filtered object
\mathcal{I}C
is given by

\mathcal{I}C^{\scalebox{.6}{$\bigstar$}}\simeq\map_{\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})}(\mathbb{S}^{\scalebox{.6}{$\bigstar$}}_{[\scalebox{.6}{$\bigstar$}]},C).

Our next goal is to show that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
factors through the localization

\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}\mathrm{Sp}\to\hbox{\hskip .
In order to do so, it will be useful to identify its graded pieces

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}(-)^{n}
.

Lemma 3.8

There is a natural equivalence

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}(-)^{n}\simeq\gr^{n}(-)[n]

of functors
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}(\mathrm{Sp})\to\mathrm{Sp}
.

Proof.

By virtue of Lemma
A.18
, it suffices to prove that both
functors preserve colimits and agree on elements of the form

\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{-}
.

As both
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathrm{ev}_{n}
admit right adjoints (see Proposition

2.12
),
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}(-)^{n}\simeq\mathrm{ev}_{n}\circ\ \widehat{\mathcal{A}}
preserves
colimits.
By Definition
2.5
,
\gr^{n}
is given by
\cofib\circ(\iota_{n})^{*}
; by

[
Lur17
, 1.1.1.8]
,
\cofib
admits a right adjoint, and since
\mathrm{Sp}
is
complete,
(\iota_{n})^{*}
admits a right adjoint, given by right Kan extension
along
\iota_{n}
.

It follows from Remark
A.16
that for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{m})\simeq\lvert\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{m}\rvert_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\simeq\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(m)\simeq\mathbb{S}^{m}_{[m]}

(see Appendix
A
for a detailed discussion)
and thus that

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\left(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{m}\right)^{n}\simeq\begin{cases}\mathbb{S}[n]\quad&\text{if }m=n,\\
0\quad&\text{if }m\neq n,\end{cases}

whereas a direct check shows that

\gr^{n}\left(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{m}\right)\simeq\begin{cases}\mathbb{S}\quad&\text{if }m=n,\\
0\quad&\text{if }m\neq n,\end{cases}

concluding the proof.
∎

Proposition 3.9

The adjunction
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\dashv\mathcal{I}
given in Definition
3.6

factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}\mathrm{Sp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hskip {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}

\dashv

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}

\dashv

through the localization of Proposition

.

Proof.

Let us first show that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
factors through the localization.
By virtue of Proposition
2.21
,
it is enough to show that it sends maps inducing equivalences on associated

,
the functor

is conservative.
It is thus enough to know that on each component,

sends local maps to equivalences, which is true by virtue of Lemma

.
As
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
preserves colimits, and
\hbox{\hskip is a full

closed under colimits (see Proposition

), we get that
\mathcal{A}
preserves colimits, and
hence admits a right adjoint.
Since adjoints compose, this just means that
\mathcal{I}
takes values
in the full subcategory of complete objects.
∎

Our next goal is to prove that the induced adjunction
\mathcal{A}\dashv\mathcal{I}

is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories. To this end, we need to prove a few key lemmata
before, and to get a better understanding of the functor
\mathcal{I}
.

Lemma 3.10

The functor
\mathcal{A}
is conservative.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\colon F\to G
be a map in
