}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}\right)^{\heartsuit}\simeq\operatorname{Ch}^{*}\left({C}^{\heartsuit}\right).

Remark 5.15

It follows from Definition
5.3
together with Corollary

5.12
that for any
F\in\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}
and
C\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
we have

\mathcal{A}\left(\tau^{B}_{\leqslant n}F\right)\simeq\tau^{\text{lvl}}_{\leqslant n}(\mathcal{A}F)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{I}\left(\tau^{\text{lvl}}_{\leqslant n}C\right)\simeq\tau^{B}_{\leqslant n}(\mathcal{I}C)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and similar formulae for the truncations above
n
.
Moreover, by Remark
5.4
, we also have

\pi^{B}_{n}F\simeq\pi^{\text{lvl}}_{n}\mathcal{A}F\quad\text{and}\quad\pi^{\text{lvl}}_{n}C\simeq\pi^{B}_{n}\mathcal{I}C

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Remark 5.16

One can construct on
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
the glued t-structure as in
Theorem
5.10
regardless of the right separatedness
hypothesis;
if
{C}
is not right separated, the class of coconnective objects will be
the full subcategory

\{F\in\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}_{\leqslant 0}\ |\ \widehat{\mathcal{A}}F\in(\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C})_{\leqslant 0}\text{ and }F^{-\infty}\simeq 0\}

but this class will in general not coincide with the one described in
Definition
5.5

(and, as by
[
Lur17
, 1.2.1.3]
the class of connective objects completely
determines the t-structure, there cannot exist
a t-structure exactly as in Definition
5.5
if
the two classes of “candidate coconnective objects” do not coincide).
We are not aware of any application for this t-structure.

6.
Symmetric monoidal structures

The
\infty
-categorical Day convolution (introduced in
[
Gla13
]
and
further developed in
[
Lur17
, 2.2.6]
) provides a way
to equip with a symmetric monoidal structure any functor category

\Fun({C},{D})
, provided that both
{C}
and
{D}
are symmetric
monoidal, and
{D}
is presentably so.
In particular (see Remark
6.1
),
we can endow
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
with a symmetric monoidal structure whenever

{C}
is presentably symmetric monoidal.
As it turns out, such monoidal structure induces one on
\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}
, and
thus on
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
, whenever Theorem
4.7
applies.
In this section, we analyze these induced symmetric monoidal structures,
and their interaction with the t-structures introduced
in the previous section. In particular, we prove that the Day convolution
structure on both
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
and
\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}
is compatible with
Beilinson t-structures, and that the symmetric monoidal structure on

\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
provides a homotopy coherent refinement of the usual tensor
product of cochain complexes.

Remark 6.1

Let
{C}
be a presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category.
By
[
Gla13
, 2.11]
(see also
[
Lur17
, 2.2.6.17]
, with
\kappa

chosen to be the strongly inaccessible cardinal determining the size of our
universe of small sets) we can endow
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}({C})
with the structure of a
symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category, given by Day convolution (where the symmetric
monoidal structure on
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}
is given by addition).
Again, by
[
Lur17
, 2.2.6.17]
, if
F
and
G
are filtered objects in

{C}
, their Day convolution product is given by Kan extension of

\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\circ(F,G)
along
+
, hence by
[
Lur09
, 4.3.3.2]
and

[
Lur09
, 4.3.2.2]
it is pointwise given by

(9)

(F\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}G)^{n}\simeq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,t)\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}\\
s+t\geqslant n\end{subarray}}F^{s}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits G^{t}

where the shape of the colimit follows from inspection
of the comma category
(+\downarrow n)
(see Definition
A.1
).
In particular,
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}({C})
is presentably symmetric monoidal.
Similarly, we can endow
\prod{Z}{C}\simeq\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\delta},{C})
with
a presentably symmetric monoidal structure given by Day convolution, whose
product is pointwise given by

\left((X_{u})_{u\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}(Y_{v})_{v\in\mathbb{Z}}\right)_{n}\simeq\bigoplus_{s+t=n}X_{s}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits Y_{t}

where once again the shape of the colimit follows from inspection
of the comma category
(+\downarrow n)
.

Proposition 6.2

Let
{C}
be a presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category, whose unit we’ll denote
by
\mathbbm{1}
. Then, the unit object for
(\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C},\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}})
is given by the filtered object

\mathbbm{1}_{\langle\leqslant 0\rangle}\coloneqq\cdots\to 0\to 0\to\mathbbm{1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}\mathbbm{1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}\cdots

consisting of copies of
\mathbbm{1}
and identity morphisms for
n\leqslant 0
,
and of copies of
0
for
n>0
.

Proof.

First, let us note that it is enough to prove the following claim:

(
\spadesuit
)

For any
F\in\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
and any
n\in\mathbb{Z}

we have

(F\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}\mathbbm{1}_{\langle\leqslant 0\rangle})^{n}\simeq F^{n}.

In fact, the existence of the unitor equivalence

\mathbbm{1}_{\text{Day}}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\mathbbm{1}_{\langle\leqslant 0\rangle}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbbm{1}_{\langle\leqslant 0\rangle}

together with the claim implies the existence of equivalences

\mathbbm{1}_{\text{Day}}^{n}\simeq\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\text{Day}}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\mathbbm{1}_{\langle\leqslant 0\rangle}\right)^{n}\simeq\mathbbm{1}_{\langle\leqslant 0\rangle}^{n}

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
As equivalences in
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
can be cheched pointwise, this is enough to
conclude

\mathbbm{1}_{\text{Day}}\simeq\mathbbm{1}_{\langle\leqslant 0\rangle}
.

We now turn to the proof of claim (
\spadesuit
).
By (
9
), this boils down to proving that the colimit
of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

is equivalent to
F^{n}
. By finality, it is enough to check that the colimit
of the following diagram

(10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

is equivalent to
F^{n}
.
If we denote by
A
the colimit of the diagram

(11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

and by
B
the colimit of the diagram

(12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{n+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

we have that, by
[
Lur09
, 4.2.3.10]
, we can decompose the colimit of
(
10
) as the coproduct
A\oplus B
. As (
11
)
consists only of zero objects, its colimit is zero. Thus, the colimit of
(
10
) is equivalent to the colimit of (
12
).
By finality, we can omit the top right arrow
0\to F^{n}
from the diagram in
order to compute its colimit.
By applying inductively
[
Lur09
, 4.2.3.10]
, we see that
B
can be computed
as the iterated pushout

F^{n}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{F^{n+1}}F^{n+1}\mathop{\amalg}\displaylimits_{F^{n+2}}F^{n+2}\cdots.

As each of the pushouts is taken along an equivalence, we have
B\simeq F^{n}
,
as desired.
∎

Recall the following definition.

Definition 6.3

[
Lur17
, 2.2.1.7]

Let
{C}
be a symmmetric monoidal
\infty
-category, and let
L\colon{C}\to{C}

be a localization functor. The functor
L
is
compatible with the
symmetric monoidal structure
if for every
L
-equivalence

X\to Y
, and every
Z\in{C}
, the induced
X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits Z\to Y\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits Z

is an
L
-equivalence.

The following proposition already appeared as
[
GP18
, 2.25]
,
we present an alternative proof.

Proposition 6.4

Let
{C}
be a presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category. Then,
the localization functor
L\colon\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}\to\hbox{\hskip 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}
is compatible with
Day convolution.

Proof.

Note that, by
[
Nik16
, 3.11]
,
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
admits an internal
mapping object given by

n\mapsto\innmap(F,G)(n)\simeq\int_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}}\map_{{C}}(F(m),G(m+n))

(where
\map_{{C}}
denotes the internal mapping object of
{C}
)
By
[
Nik16
, 2.12 (3)]
, it suffices to prove that, given any

F\in\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
and any
G\in\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}
, the internal mapping object is
complete; by the following computation

\begin{split}\lim_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}}\ \innmap(F,G)(n)&\simeq\lim_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}}\int_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}}\map_{{C}}\bigg(F(m),G(m+n)\bigg)\\
&\simeq\int_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}}\lim_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}}\map_{{C}}\bigg(F(m),G(m+n)\bigg)\\
&\simeq\int_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}}\map_{{C}}\bigg(F(m),\lim_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}}G(m+n)\bigg)\\
&\simeq\int_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}}\map_{{C}}\bigg(F(m),0\bigg)\\
&\simeq\vphantom{\int}0.\end{split}

this is in fact the case.
∎

Remark 6.5

[
GP18
, 2.25]

It follows from Proposition
6.4
and

[
Lur17
, 2.2.1.9]
that if
{C}
is presentably symmetric monoidal,
we have an induced presentably symmetric monoidal structure on

\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern }$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}({C})
, which we’ll refer to as the
completed Day convolution

monoidal structure and
will denote by
\widehat{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits}
. In particular, we have that

F\ \widehat{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits}\ G\simeq L\Big(F\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}G\Big).

Remark 6.6

From Proposition
6.4
and
Remark
6.5
, we have
that the unit for
\widehat{\mathop{\otimes}}\displaylimits
is given by

L\mathbbm{1}_{\langle\leqslant 0\rangle}\simeq\mathbbm{1}_{\langle\leqslant 0\rangle}.

Definition 6.7

Let
{C}
be a stable presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category.
We refer to the symmetric monoidal structure induced on
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
by the
equivalence of Theorem
4.7
as the
coherent
cochains tensor product
, and we will denote it just by
\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits
.

Remark 6.8

It follows from Theorem
4.7
together with
Remark
4.9

that the functor
u
defined in Lemma

2.11

fits into the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod{Z}{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod{Z}{C}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Sigma^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}}

that is,
u\circ\widehat{\mathcal{A}}F\simeq\left(\gr^{n}F[n]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}

naturally in
F\in\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
.

The symmetric monoidal stucture given in Definition

6.7
is really a homotopy coherent generalization
of the usual tensor product of cochain complexes, in a sense made precise
by the following results.

Proposition 6.9

The functor
u\circ\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is symmetric monoidal.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
[
GP18
, 2.26]
together
with Remark
6.8
and
(\Sigma^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}

being an equivalence.
∎

Corollary 6.10

Let
{C}
be a stable presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category.
Let
C
and
D
be elements of
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
. Then, we have that

(C\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits D)^{n}\simeq\bigoplus_{s+t=n}C^{s}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{C}D^{t}.

Proof.

By definition of
u
(see Lemma

2.11
),

(C\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits D)^{n}\simeq u(C\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits D)^{n}
. The result follows from
the following computation

\begin{split}u(C\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits D)^{n}&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{thm-cohch-fild-equiv})}}}{{\simeq}}u\Big(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}C\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\mathcal{A}\mathcal{I}D\Big)^{n}\\
&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{defi-coh-tensor-product-coch})}}}{{\simeq}}u\mathcal{A}\Big(\mathcal{I}C\ \widehat{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits}\ \mathcal{I}D\Big)^{n}\\
&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{rem-day-localized})}}}{{\simeq}}u\mathcal{A}L\Big(\mathcal{I}C\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}\mathcal{I}D\Big)^{n}\\
&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{prop-imp-is-complete})}}}{{\simeq}}u\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\Big(\mathcal{I}C\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}\mathcal{I}D\Big)^{n}\\
&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{prop-undch-aush-symm})}}}{{\simeq}}\Big(u\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{I}C\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}u\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{I}D\Big)^{n}\\
&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{prop-counit-almost-equiv})}}}{{\simeq}}\vphantom{\Big(}\Big(uC\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}uD\Big)^{n}\\
&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{rem-day-structures})}}}{{\simeq}}\bigoplus_{s+t=n}C^{s}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{{C}}D^{t}.\end{split}

∎

We now analyze the interaction between the symmetric monoidal structures
introduced in this section, and the t-structures introduced in Section

5
. We start by recalling the following definition
(see
[
Lur17
, 2.2.1]
and
[
AN20
, A.2]
for more details about the
general theory of the interaction between t-structures and symmetric monoidal
structures).

Definition 6.11

Let
{C}
be a stably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category equipped with a t-structure

({C}_{\leqslant 0},{C}_{\geqslant 0})
.
The t-structure is said to be
compatible
with the symmetric monoidal
structure if the following conditions hold:

(1)

The unit object for
\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits
lies in
{C}_{\geqslant 0}
;

(2)

Given any pair of connective objects
X,Y\in{C}_{\geqslant 0}
,
their product
X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits Y
lies in
{C}_{\geqslant 0}
as well.

Remark 6.12

Conditions (
1
) and (
2
) guarantee that

{C}_{\geqslant 0}
inherits a symmetric monoidal structure from
{C}

such that the fully faithful inclusion

{C}_{\geqslant 0}\hookrightarrow{C}
is a (strong) symmetric monidal functor.

Proposition 6.13

Let
{C}
be a presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category equipped with
a t-structure that is compatible with the monoidal structure. Then:

(1)

The Beilinson t-structure on
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
is compatible with
the Day convolution product
\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}
;

(2)

The Beilinson t-structure on
\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}{C}
is compatible
with the completed Day convolution product
\widehat{\mathop{\otimes}}\displaylimits
;

(3)

If
{C}
is moreover stable, the pointwise
t-structure on
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
is compatible with the coherent cochains
tensor product
\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits

Proof.

By Proposition
6.2
, we have that

\gr^{i}\mathbbm{1}_{\langle\leqslant 0\rangle}\simeq\begin{cases}0&\text{ for }i\neq 1;\\
\mathbbm{1}&\text{ for }i=1.\end{cases}

and thus the unit is Beilinson connective.
Let us now consider
F,G\in\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}_{\geqslant 0}
.
By
[
GP18
, 2.26]
we have that

\gr^{i}(F\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}G)\simeq\bigoplus_{s+t=i}\gr^{s}F\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\gr^{t}G

from which we immediately have that
F\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}G
lies in

(\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C})_{\geqslant 0}
. This completes the proof of (
1
).
By Proposition
2.21
, applying the localization
functor
L
has no effect on associated gradeds, hence (
1
)
immediately implies (
2
). Finally,
(
3
) is a trivial consequence of
(
2
) and the definition of
\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits
on
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
.
∎

Remark 6.14

It follows from
[
Lur17
, 2.2.1.10]
that Day convolution, and hence also the
tensor product of coherent chain complexes, induce a symmetric monoidal
structure on the hearts of the respective Beilinson t-structures.
From Corollary
6.10
, we have
that the induced symmetric monoidal structure on

\hbox{\hskip 412.8906pt\hskip-15.45145pt\hbox{$\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}$}\hskip-15.45145pt\hskip-412.8906pt\raisebox{3.43446pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.33835pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{507903}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}
}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}(\mathrm{Sp})^{\heartsuit}\simeq\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})^{\heartsuit}\simeq\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathbb{Z})

is the usual one.

7.
Coherent cochain complexes and Toda brackets

In this section, we will have a closer look at the relation between
Toda brackets and coherent cochain complexes.
Our main result will be characterizing coherent cochain complexes in suitable
pointed
\infty
-categories as being precisely the sequences of objects

\cdots\to X^{n}\xrightarrow{f^{n}}X^{n+1}\xrightarrow{f^{n+1}}X^{n+2}\cdots

such that all pairwise composable morphisms are nullhomotopic (i.e. for all

n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
f^{n+1}\circ f^{n}\simeq 0
) and all possible Toda brackets
are compatibly trivial (see Remark
7.20
).

Let us first start with a short recollection about Toda brackets.
Given a pointed
\infty
-category
{C}
, let

f^{0}\colon X^{0}\to X^{1}
,
f^{1}\colon X^{1}\to X^{2}
and
f^{2}\colon X^{2}\to X^{3}

be morphisms in
{C}
, such that
f^{1}f^{0}\simeq f^{2}f^{1}\simeq 0
, and let

\alpha\colon 0\Rightarrow f^{1}f^{0}
and
\beta\colon 0\Rightarrow f^{1}f^{2}
be
two choices of nullhomotopies:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{3}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox{90.0}{$\Rightarrow$}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rotatebox{90.0}{$\Leftarrow$}\beta}

In such a situation, the two whiskerings
f^{2}\alpha
and
\beta f^{0}
determine
two paths
0\Rightarrow f^{2}f^{1}f^{0}
in
\Map_{*}(X^{0},X^{3})
.
By gluing them along their endpoints, we obtain a map (pointed at zero)

T\colon S^{1}\to\Map_{*}(X^{0},X^{3})
, which in turns determines a class

\langle f^{2},f^{1},f^{0}\rangle_{(\alpha,\beta)}\in\pi_{1}(\Map(X^{0},X^{3}))
.
The homotopy class
\langle f^{2},f^{1},f^{0}\rangle_{(\alpha,\beta)}
is known as
the Toda bracket determined by
\alpha
and
\beta
.

The customary approach to
Toda brackets would be not fix
\alpha
and
\beta
as part of the datum,
and to instead define the Toda bracket to be a subset of
\pi_{1}(\Map(X^{0},X^{3}))
,
whose elements are given by all the possible choices of homotopy classes of
paths
([\alpha],[\beta])
. According to this definition, the object
referred above as
\langle h,g,f\rangle_{(\alpha,\beta)}
is a specified
element of this subset.

The notion can be generalized to longer sequences of maps, provided that the
Toda brackets of the shorter sub-sequences are nullhomotopic; to illustrate how
the generalization works, let us consider the case of a sequence of length 4

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{4}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{3}}

and choices of nullhomotopies
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
for all consecutive
pairs of maps, for which
\langle f^{2},f^{1},f^{0}\rangle_{(\alpha,\beta)}=0

and
\langle f^{3},f^{2},f^{1}\rangle_{(\beta,\gamma)}=0
; in such a situation,
we obtain a pair of nullhomotopies for a pair of maps
X^{0}\to\Sigma X^{4}
, one
for the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma X^{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle f^{3},f^{2},f^{1}\rangle_{(\beta,\gamma)}}

and the other one for composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma X^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma X^{4};}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle f^{2},f^{1},f^{0}\rangle_{(\alpha,\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma f^{3}}

putting together these two as before, we obtain a pointed map

S^{1}\to\Map_{*}(X^{0},\Sigma X^{4})
, and thus a class

\langle f^{3},f^{2},f^{1},f^{0}\rangle\in\pi_{2}(\Map_{*}(X^{0},X^{3}))
,
the
4-fold Toda bracket
of the
sequence
11
11

11

again, we are working with a coherent notion, depending also
on the choices of all the five nullhomotopies involved
.
This idea clearly generalizes to longer sequences of maps,
provided that the shorter subsequences have nullhomotopic brackets.

Although it is possible to work with these notions using the approach sketched
above, in the rest of the section we will use a
slightly different perspective to define and generalize Toda brackets.
Our approach will be to define these classes by means
of the actions of certain algebra objects in graded pointed spaces;
the main advantage for us will be the ease of working
at once with all the possible
n
-fold Toda brackets in a
\mathbb{Z}
-indexed sequence
(provided that all the possible
(n-1)
-fold Toda brackets are
trivial);
of course, one can recover the case of finite sequences
by considering a
\mathbb{Z}
-indexed sequence where only finitely many
objects are non-zero (see Definition
7.16
for the details).
One other pleasant feature
of our approach will be to obtain an inherently coherent notion of Toda brackets
(that is, encoding also choices for all the involved homotopies, rather than
defining the notion up to a coset of “indeterminacies”).

Our strategy for proving that coherent chain complexes are precisely those

\mathbb{Z}
-indexed sequences of morphisms where consecutive maps compose to zero
and all Toda brackets are coherently nullhomotopic requires a few steps:
in §
7.1
,
we will prove that for a pointed
\infty
-category
{C}

(which we will for simplicity assume to be also presentable) both

\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
12
12

12

in the present section we will use the notation

\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
instead of the previously introduced
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}({C})

to stress that here we want to think not of structured
objects of independent interest, but of sequences
of morphisms upon which we intend to put relations;
in particular, the reader should
keep in mind that the appearence of this
\infty
-category here has nothing to do with
the equivalence of Theorem
4.7

and
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
are

\infty
-categories of modules for suitable
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra objects in
\Gr{S}_{*}
.
Then, in §
7.2

we will construct a sequence of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebras in
\Gr{S}_{*}

(13)

R_{1}\to R_{2}\to R_{3}\to\cdots

such that the
\infty
-category of modules over
R_{1}
will be equivalent to

\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
, the
\infty
-category of modules over
\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{n}R_{n}
will be
equivalent to
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
, and such that the sequence induced
by (
13
) on
homology will be equivalent to the resolution of
\Lambda(e)
described in
Theorem
B.1
, with
e
of degree 1.
Modules over
R_{n}
for a fixed
n\geqslant 2
will
be sequences where all pairwise composable morphisms will be nullhomotopic,
all possible
m
-fold Toda brackets for
m\leqslant n
will be defined and trivial,
and all the relevant nullhomotopies will be encoded in the module structure
(see Remark
7.15
for the details).
In order to prove the existence of the sequence (
13
),
we will use the results of Appendix
B
.
As it turns out, the construction of the above sequence up to
R_{3}
is
slightly more subtle than its extension to all the other
R_{n}
’s,
hence we will first construct the sequence inductively for
n\geqslant 4
,
and defer the construction of the beginning of the sequence
to §
7.3
.

7.1.
Algebras in graded pointed spaces

The main goal of this section is to prove that, given a pointed
\infty
-category
{C}
,
both
\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})
and
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})
can be expressed as
\infty
-categories of
modules over suitably defined
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebras in graded pointed spaces.
For simplicity, we will assume that
{C}
is also presentable, but with
some extra effort the results can be generalized further, using methods
along the lines of those employed in Section
4
.

The stable analogue of the following proposition appeared as

[
Lur15
, 2.4.4]
.

Proposition 7.1

Let
{C}
be a pointed
\infty
-category. The following data are equivalent:

(1)

self-equivalences
{C}\to{C}
;

(2)

(left or right) actions of
\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}
on
{C}
;

(3)

monoidal functors
\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}\to\Fun({C},{C})
;

(4)

monoidal functors
\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}\to\Fun^{\text{Rex}}({C},{C})
;

(5)

right exact monoidal functors

\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{\delta})\to\Fun^{\text{Rex}}({C},{C})
;

(6)

(left or right) actions of
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{\delta})
on
{C}

such that the action map commutes with finite colimits in each variable.

x

If
{C}
is pointed presentable, then the above are equivalent to:

x

(7)

left adjoint monoidal functors

\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{\delta})\to\Fun^{\text{Rex}}({C},{C})
;

(8)

actions of
\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{\delta})
on
{C}
such that the action
map commutes with small colimits in each variable.

Here
\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{\delta})
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{\delta})
are defined as in §
A.3
.

Proof.

The equivalence (
1
)
\Leftrightarrow

(
3
) follows
from
[
Lur15
, 2.4.3]
:
loc. cit.
gives an equivalence

\Fun^{\otimes}\kern-1.99997pt\left(\mathbb{Z}^{\delta},\Fun({C},{C})\right)\simeq\Aut({C})

where the monoidal structure on
\Fun({C},{C})
is given by composition,
and
\Aut({C})\subset\Fun({C},{C})
denotes the full subcategory spanned
by self-equivalences.
(
3
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
2
) and
(
5
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
6
) are just
reformulations of the definition of action of a
symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category.
(
3
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
4
) holds trivially, as

\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}
is a discrete category.
(
4
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
5
) follows from
Proposition
A.28
and the monoidality of the pointed
Yoneda embedding (see
[
Nik16
, §6]
).
(
7
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
5
) and
(
8
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
6
)
follow at once from Proposition
A.28
.
∎

Remark 7.2

In what follows, unless otherwise specified, we will implicitly work with
left actions whenever we apply Proposition
7.1
.
Recall that, in the terminology of
[
Lur17
]
, a left action of a monoidal
\infty
-category
{C}
on some
\infty
-category
{M}
is equivalent to exhibiting
{M}
as
left-tensored over
{C}
, and to exhibiting
{M}
as a left
{C}
-module
in (a suitably sized)
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories (see
[
Lur15
, p. 8]
).

Example 7.3

Let
{C}
be a pointed
\infty
-category. Then, precomposition with

-1\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
induces an automorphism

(-)\{1\}\colon\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}\ \colon C^{\bullet}\mapsto C\{1\}^{\bullet}\simeq C^{\bullet-1};

by virtue of Proposition

7.1
, this endowes
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
with the structure of
an
\infty
-category left-tensored over
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{\delta})\simeq\Gr({S}_{*}^{\text{fin}})

(and when
{C}
is also presentable, the left-tensoring extends over

\Gr({S}_{*})
);
we will use the notation:

(-)\{n\}\colon\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}

to denote the (right exact) endofunctor induced by
n
.

Example 7.4

Let
{C}
be a pointed
\infty
-category.
Similarly to the previous example, precomposition with

-1\colon\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}
induces an automorphism

(-)\{1\}\colon\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}\to\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C};

hence,
\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}
is also left-tensored over

\Gr({S}_{*}^{\text{fin}})

(and when
{C}
is also presentable, the left-tensoring extends over

\Gr({S}_{*})
);
also in this case, we will use the notation:

(-)\{n\}\colon\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}\to\mathrm{Fil}^{\text{\scalebox{0.85}{$\downarrow$}}}{C}\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}

to denote the automorphism induced by
n
.

Notation 7.5

Given any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, we will denote by

S^{n,t}\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{S}_{*}
the graded pointed space consisting of a copy of

S^{n}
in degree
t
, and copies of
\mathrm{pt}
in all other degrees.

Proposition 7.6

Let
C\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
be a coherent cochain complex in a complete
13
13

13

or,
more generally, in a pointed
\infty
-category where all the relevant right Kan extensions
exist and are pointwise
, pointed
\infty
-category.
The functor
-\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C\colon\Gr{S}_{*}\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
induced by
the left-tensoring of Example
7.3
admits a right adjoint,
denoted
\innmap{}_{*}(C,-)
,
whose value on any
D\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}
is pointwise given by

\innmap{}_{*}(C,D)^{n}\simeq\Map{}_{*}\left(C\{n\},D\right).

Proof.

By unraveling the definitions, the
\Gr{S}_{*}
-action
on
\Fun^{\text{Rex}}(\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C},\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C})
is obtained by Kan
extension of the functor

\{\}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}\to\Fun^{\text{Rex}}(\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C},\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C})\colon n\mapsto\{n\}
along the functor
y\colon\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}\to\Gr{S}_{*}\colon n\mapsto S^{0,n}
. As the Kan extension is pointwise,

-\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C\simeq\Lan_{y}(\mathrm{ev}_{C}\circ\{\})

where
\mathrm{ev}_{C}\colon\Fun^{\text{Rex}}(\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C},\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C})\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}

denotes the evaluation at
C
functor.
In particular, if we put
F\coloneqq\mathrm{ev}_{C}\circ\{\}
, we have that

-\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C
is, in the language of Appendix
A
,
the pointed
F
-realization functor. Thus, it admits as a right adjoint
given by the pointed
F
-nerve functor
\nerve_{F}^{\text{pt}}
,
which by Proposition
A.29
is given by

\nerve_{F}^{\text{pt}}(D)\simeq\Map{}_{*}(F(-),D)\simeq\Map{}_{*}\left(C\{-\},D\right)

as desired.
∎

Definition 7.7

We will refer to the right adjoint functor defined in Proposition

7.6

as the
graded pointed mapping space
functor.

Theorem 7.8

Let
{C}
be a pointed presentable
\infty
-category. Then,
there exists an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra in graded pointed spaces

A\in\mathrm{Alg}_{\Gr{S}_{*}}

such that the
\infty
-category of coherent cochain
complexes in
{C}
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of right
A
-modules
in graded objects of
{C}
:

\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{C}\simeq\operatorname{RMod}_{A}(\Gr{C}).

Moreover, the underlying graded pointed space of
A
is given by

S^{0,0}\amalg S^{0,1}
.

Proof.

Let us first notice that the claim for a generic pointed presentable

\infty
-category
{C}
can be deduced from the case
{C}\simeq{S}_{*}
,
by means of Lurie’s tensor product (see
[
Lur17
, §4.8]
):
by
[
Lur17
, 4.8.1.17]
,

\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})\simeq\Fun(\mathbf{Ch}^{\mathrm{op}},{S}_{*})\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits{C};

since preserving the zero object is a property, the equivalence above induces
an equivalence
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})\simeq\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({S}_{*})\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits{C}
.
Now, by
[
Lur17
, 4.8.4.6]
,

\operatorname{RMod}_{A}(\Gr{S}_{*})\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\Gr{S}_{*}}{C}\simeq\operatorname{RMod}_{A}(\Gr{C});

it thus suffices to prove our claim for the case of pointed spaces.

By Example
7.3
, together with Proposition

7.1
, we have that
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{S}_{*}
is left-tensored
over
\Gr{S}_{*}
(see Remark
7.2
).
By
[
Lur17
, 4.8.5.8]
, it is thus enough to prove that:

(1)

\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({S}_{*})
admits geometric realizations of simplicial
objects.

(2)

The action map
\Gr{S}_{*}\times\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({S}_{*})\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({S}_{*})
preserves geometric realizations of simplicial objects.

(3)

There exists an
M\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({S}_{*})
such that
the functor
-\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits M\colon\Gr{S}_{*}\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({S}_{*})

admits a right adjoint, denoted
\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,-)
.

(4)

The functor
\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,-)
preserves geometric realizations
of simplicial objects.

(5)

The functor
\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,-)
is conservative.

(6)

For every coherent chain complex
C
and every graded pointed space

X
, the map

X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,C)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits M\xrightarrow{X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\varepsilon_{C}}X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C

is adjoint to an equivalence

X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,C)\to\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C).

We take
M
to be the coherent cochain complex consisting of a copy of

S^{0}
sitting in degree
0
, a copy of
S^{0}
sitting in degree
1
,
copies of
\mathrm{pt}
elsewhere, and
the identity as its only possibly nontrivial differential.
(
1
) follows from the cocompleteness of

{S}_{*}
.
(
2
) follows from presentability of

{S}_{*}
and Proposition
7.1
.
(
3
) and
(
4
) hold for any choice of
M
, and
follow from Proposition
7.6
.
Using the explicit description for the right adjoint given in Proposition

7.6
, we get that for our choice of

M
,
\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,C)^{n}\simeq\Map{}_{*}(M\{n\},C)

is equivalent to the space of choices of
f
and
g
’s making the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial^{n}}

commute; this space is in turn equivalent to
\Map{}_{*}(S^{0},C^{n})\simeq C^{n}
,
and thus the functor
\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,-)
is equivalent to the
forgetful functor

u\colon\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{S}_{*}\to\Gr{S}_{*}
introduced in Lemma

2.11
,
implying (
5
).

For (
6
), by definition of adjoint map,
the adjoint map of interest can be factored as

X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,C)\xrightarrow{\eta_{X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,C)}}\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,C)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits M)\xrightarrow{\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\varepsilon_{C})}

\xrightarrow{\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\varepsilon_{C})}\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C).

By a pointwise check, we have that

\eta_{X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,C)}\simeq\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,\eta_{X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C})
,
and
X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\varepsilon_{c}\simeq\varepsilon_{X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C}
;
by the conservativity of
\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,-)
,
it is sufficient to show that the composite

X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C\xrightarrow{\eta_{X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C}}\underline{\Map}{}_{*}(M,X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits M\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C}}X\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits C

is an equivalence;
but, as the latter is one of the triangle identities, the desired condition
holds.

Finally, in the proof of
[
Lur17
, 4.8.5.8]
is shown that
A
can be
identified with the internal hom object

\innmap{}_{*}(M,M)
,
thence we get the desired characterization for the graded pointed
space underlying
A
.
∎

Remark 7.9

As, by
[
GGN15
, 5.5]
, the stabilization functor is symmetric
monoidal on presentable
\infty
-categories, we can stabilize Theorem

7.8
, to obtain an equivalence

\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({D})\simeq\operatorname{RMod}_{\mathbb{S}^{0,0}\oplus\mathbb{S}^{0,1}}(\Gr{D})

for any stable presentable
\infty
-category
{D}
.

Theorem 7.10

Let
{C}
be a pointed presentable
\infty
-category. Then,
the
\infty
-category of filtered objects of
{C}
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of right

\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})
-modules in graded objects:

\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})\simeq\operatorname{RMod}_{\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})}(\Gr{C}).

Proof.

Let us first prove the claim for the case
{C}\simeq{S}_{*}
.
This is basically an adaptation of
[
Lur15
, 3.1.6]
to
the case of pointed spaces.

First of all, notice that, as the functor

u\colon\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{S}_{*})\to\Gr{S}_{*}

given by precomposition with
\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}\to\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}\colon n\mapsto-n

is symmetric monoidal, it induces a functor

\theta\colon\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{S}_{*})\simeq\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbbm{1}_{\text{Day}}}(\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{S}_{*}))\to\mathrm{Mod}_{u\mathbbm{1}_{\text{Day}}}(\Gr{S}_{*});

by Proposition
6.2
together with
[
Lur17
, 4.1.1.18]
, we have
that
u\mathbbm{1}_{\langle\leqslant 0\rangle}
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra
whose underlying
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra is equivalent to

\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})
.

It is thus sufficient to show that the functor
\theta
is an equivalence.
Let us start with fully faithfulness.
That is, we want to prove that the maps

\varphi_{X,Y}\colon\Map{}_{*}(X,Y)\to\Map{}_{*}(\theta X,\theta Y)

are all equivalences.
As the functor
\varphi_{-,Y}\colon\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{S}_{*})\to\Fun(\Delta^{1},{S}_{*})
sending a graded pointed space
X
to the map

\varphi_{X,Y}
sends colimits to limits, the full subcategory spanned by those

X
such that
\varphi_{X,Y}
is an equivalence is a pointed subcategory of

\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{S}_{*})
closed under colimits.
Thus, as
\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{S}_{*})
is generated under colimits by objects
of the form
\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}^{\raisebox{-3.18884pt}{\scalebox{.7}{\kern-2.3917ptpt}}}_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
(see Proposition

A.28
), it suffices to show that

\varphi_{-,Y}
is an equivalence for such generators;
this is indeed the case, as for
X\simeq\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}^{\raisebox{-3.18884pt}{\scalebox{.7}{\kern-2.3917ptpt}}}_{n}
we have

\varphi_{X,Y}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{Y^{n}}
.
Essential surjectivity now follows from the observation that the essential
image of
\theta
is closed under colimits, as
\theta
is fully faithful
and commutes with colimits.

Similarly to the proof of Theorem
7.8
,
the claim for general pointed presentable
\infty
-categories follows from
the properties of Lurie’s tensor product.
By
[
Lur17
, 4.8.1.17]
,

\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{C})\simeq\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{S}_{*})\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits{C}

and by
[
Lur17
, 4.8.4.6]
,

\operatorname{RMod}_{\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})}(\Gr{S}_{*})\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\Gr{S}_{*}}{C}\simeq\operatorname{RMod}_{\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})}(\Gr{C})

we thence have the desired claim.
∎

Remark 7.11

We can describe more explicitly the equivalence of Theorem

7.10
.
Let us first observe that the action of
the free generator of
R_{1}\coloneqq\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})
determines
the action of all other spaces of
R_{1}
.
By unraveling the definitions, specifying a map

t\colon S^{0,1}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits X\to X
on a graded object
X
is equivalent to
specifying maps
X^{n}\to X^{n+1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence, given an
R_{1}
-module, the associated filtered object is the one
having as maps between consecutive objects the maps determined by
t
,
and the actions of the
s
-th powers of the generator

t^{s}\colon S^{0,s}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits X\to X
correspond to
choices for all the possible
s
-fold composites for the structure
maps of the resulting filtered object (i.e. maps

X^{n}\to X^{n+s}
for all
n>1
). This description makes
also clear the behavior of the functor going in the opposite direction.

7.2.
The resolution sequence

We will now move to the construction of the sequence
(
13
). By Theorem

7.10
, we can take

R_{1}\simeq\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})
.
We will define the first map of the sequence to be the one obtained by
killing the square of the free generator of
R_{1}
.

Construction 7.12

By
[
Lur17
, 4.1.1.18]
, we have

\left(\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})\right)^{n}\simeq S^{n}
for all
n\geqslant 0
.
In particular, the inclusion of pointed spaces

S^{0,2}\to\mathrm{Free}(S^{0,1})
induces a map of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebras

t^{2}\colon\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,2})\to\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})
.
We define
R_{2}
to be the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra in graded pointed spaces
obtained as the pushout of
t^{2}
along the augmentation map for the
free algebra
\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,2})
:

(14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{0,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{2}}

We pick the bottom horizontal map
\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})\simeq R_{1}\to R_{2}
to be the first map in the sequence
(
13
).

Remark 7.13

Notice that, as passing to homology preserves colimits, the pushout
square (
14
) induces on homology the square of Theorem

B.2
for the case
n=2
.

In Lemma
7.25
, we will show that there exists a map

S^{1,3}\to R_{2}
, inducing on homology the map

\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(r_{3})\to\Lambda^{(2)}
of Theorem
B.2
.
Given such a map, we can consider the pushout diagram of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{1,3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{0,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{3}}

and pick the bottom horizontal map to be the second map in the sequence
(
13
).

Similarly, in Lemma
7.27
,
we will show that there exists a map
S^{2,4}\to R_{2}
, inducing on homology
the map
\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(r_{4})\to\Lambda^{(3)}
of Theorem

B.2
.

Given such maps, we can construct the rest of the sequence by induction.

Construction 7.14

Let us assume we are given a sequence

R_{1}\to R_{2}\to\cdots\to R_{n}
with
n\geqslant 3
, such that every

R_{j}
comes equipped with a map
S^{j-1,j+1}\to R_{j}

inducing on homology the map

\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(r_{j+1})\to\Lambda^{(j)}
of
Theorem
B.2
,
and that each map
R_{j-1}\to R_{j}
for
2\leqslant j\leqslant n

fits in a commutative square of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{j-2,j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{0,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{j-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{j}.}

Our goal is to produce inductively
an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra
R_{n+1}
and a map

S^{n,n+2}\to R_{n+1}
inducing on homology the map

\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(r_{n+2})\to\Lambda^{(n+1)}
of
Theorem
B.2
.

Let us denote by
R_{n+1}
the object obtained pushing out
the map
\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{n-1,n+1})\to R_{n}
we have by inductive
hypothesis along the augmentation map of the free algebra:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{n-1,n+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{0,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{n+1}.}

We pick the bottom map of the above square to be the one for the
sequence (
13
).
By passing to homology, we get precisely the pushout square
of Theorem
B.2
.
By Lemma
B.14
, we have an element
r_{n+1}

of bidegree
(n+1,n-1)
in

\Lambda^{(n)}\simeq\Lambda(e)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits\mathbb{Z}[r_{n+1}]
.
By Hurewicz’s Theorem, the cohomology class
r_{n+1}
determines
in an essentially unique way a map
S^{n,n+2}\to R_{n+1}
, which we will
denote
\langle t\rangle^{n+2}
and call the

(n+2)
-fold Toda power of
t
.
This completes the induction step needed to extend the sequence for

m\geqslant 4
.

Remark 7.15

We can now understand the relations between
R_{n}
-modules for varying
n
:

(1)

It follows from Construction
7.12
together with
Remark
7.11
that the
R_{1}
-module associated to
a filtered space
X
can be given the structure of an
R_{2}
-module
if and only if all the composites of pairs of consecutive maps of
X

are nullhomotopic, and specifying the structure of an

R_{2}
-module is equivalent to choosing a nullhomotopy for each
such pair.

(2)

It follows from Construction
7.14
together with
Remark
7.11
that given any
R_{n-1}
-module
C
,
it can be given the structure of an
R_{n}
-module if and only if
the map
\langle t\rangle^{n}\colon S^{n-2,n}\to\innmap{}_{*}(C,C)
of
Construction
7.14

14
14

14

or,
in the case of
n=3
(resp.
n=4
), the map

\langle t\rangle^{3}
defined in Construction
7.24

(resp. the map
\langle t\rangle^{4}
defined in Construction

7.26
)

is trivial.
By unraveling the definitions, we see that if we denote
by
X
the filtered space associated to
C
(obtained by restriction of
scalars along
R_{1}\to R_{n-1}
), the components of
\langle t\rangle^{n}

are maps

S^{n-2}\to\Map_{*}(X^{m},X^{m+n})

for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
; specifying the structure of an
R_{n}
-module on
C

is equivalent to choosing nullhomotopies for each such map.

Definition 7.16

Let
X^{0}\to X^{1}\to\cdots\to X^{n}
be a sequence of pointed spaces and
maps between them, and assume that the filtered pointed space
X
obtained
by extending the given sequence by zeroes
can be given the structure of an
R_{n-1}
-module.
Let us moreover fix one such structure and denote the resulting object by

C_{X}
.
We define the
n-fold Toda bracket
of
C_{X}
to be the class

S^{n-2}\to\Map_{*}(X^{0},X^{n})

given by the only nontrivial component of the map

\langle t\rangle^{n}\colon S^{n-2,n}\to\innmap{}_{*}(C_{X},C_{X})
of Construction

7.14
, or,
in the case of
n=3
(resp.
n=4
), the map

\langle t\rangle^{3}
defined in Construction
7.24

(resp. the map
\langle t\rangle^{4}
defined in Construction

7.26
).

Definition 7.17

Given any filtered pointed space
X\in\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}},{S}_{*})
, we say
it is a
naive cochain complex
if its corresponding
R_{1}
-module
can be given a structure of
R_{2}
-module. Given a naive cochain complex

X
, we say that it has
uniformly trivial n-fold Toda
brackets
if it can be recursively given the structure of an
R_{n}
-module
after choosing
R_{m}
-module structures for all
m<n
.

Remark 7.18

As all pointed
\infty
-categories are canonically enriched over pointed spaces, the
definition of Toda brackets generalizes in an obvious way to sequences of
maps in an arbitrary pointed
\infty
-category.
In particular, it extends to sequences of maps in any stable
\infty
-category.

Proposition 7.19

The
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra
A
of Theorem
7.8

is equivalent to the colimit

\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{n}R_{n}
of the sequence (
13
);
i.e. for
{C}
pointed presentable with a special extremal separator,

\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C})\simeq\operatorname{RMod}_{\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{op}}}R_{n}}(\Gr{C}).

Proof.

It follows from the resolution sequence (
13
),
together with the associated sequence in homology, that the underlying
graded space of
\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{n}R_{n}
is equivalent to
S^{0,0}\amalg S^{0,1}
, as
is also the underlying space of the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra
A
of Theorem

7.8
. As
S^{0,0}\amalg S^{0,1}
is 0-truncated,
the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra structures on it are determined by the associative
ring structures on the discrete object
S^{0,0}\amalg S^{0,1}

(living in the 1-category
\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathbf{Top}_{*})
).
But, by a direct check, there exists only
one nontrivial associative ring structure on
S^{0,0}\amalg S^{0,1}
.
As
\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{n}R_{n}
is augmented over
S^{0,0}
, it cannot have trivial
multiplication; likewise,
A
cannot be trivial
by Theorem
7.8
. Therefore
A
and
\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{n}R_{n}

must be equivalent as
\mathbb{E}_{1}
algebras.
∎

Remark 7.20

The above proposition can be informally rephrased by saying that the datum
of a coherent cochain complex is equivalent to the datum of a naive
chain complex together with choices for all the nullhomotopies of pairs
of consecutive maps, and recursively defined choices of nullhomotopies for
all possible
n
-fold Toda brackets, for all
n\geqslant 3
.

7.3.
The resolution sequence for
n\leqslant 3

Notation 7.21

Let
{C}
be a pointed
\infty
-category. Let
X=(X^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\Gr{C}
.
Given any
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and any
t\in\mathbb{N}
will denote by

\Omega^{t,s}
the composite functor

\Omega_{C}^{t}\circ\{-s\}\simeq\{-s\}\circ\Omega_{C}^{t}.

That is,

(\Omega^{t,s}X)^{n}\simeq\Omega^{t}(X^{n+s}).

Notice that, for
S^{0,0}\in\Gr{S}_{*}
, we have

\Omega^{0,s}\left(S^{0,0}\right)\simeq S^{0,-s}
.

Remark 7.22

Let
X\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}{S}_{*}
; we have that:

(1)

For all
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\innmap{}_{*}(S^{t,s},X)\simeq(\Omega^{t}X)\{-s\}\simeq\Omega^{t,s}X;

(2)

For all
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,

S^{t,s}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\text{Day}}X\simeq(\Sigma^{t}X)\{s\}.

Remark 7.23

The structure map
\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(S^{0,1})\to R_{2}

determines a

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{2}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0,3}R_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

\Rightarrow

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

\Leftarrow

Construction 7.24

In the situation of Remark

,
let us denote by

the fiber (in

-modules) of multiplication
by

, considered as a map

.
Choosing a nullhomotopy

is equivalent to chosing a factorization of

through

.
By the following diagram (where all squares are Cartesian)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0,1}F_{2}}

we see that there exists a map

We call this map the

of

.
With a little abuse of terminology, we will reserve the same name

obtained by adjoining over

\cfrac{R_{2}\to\innmap{}_{*}(S^{1,3},R_{2})}{\cfrac{R_{2}\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits S^{1,3}\to R_{2}}{S^{1,3}\to\innmap{}_{*}(R_{2},R_{2})}}

As

is a free
R_{2}
-module of rank
1
, we have that

\innmap{}_{*}(R_{2},R_{2})\simeq R_{2}
, hence the triple Toda power

S^{1,3}\to R_{2}
.

Lemma 7.25

The map
S^{1,3}\to R_{2}
constructed above induces on homology the map

\mathrm{Free}_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(r_{3})\to\Lambda^{(2)}
defined in
Theorem

.

Proof.

Upon passing to homology degree-wise, using that

in the notation of Theorem

B.2
,
and that, by Remark
B.15
, we can use

\widetilde{P}_{2}\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle/(\partial e_{2}=e_{1}^{2})\simeq\Lambda^{(2)}

as a representative

-category of dg-modules,
the defining diagram can be represented by the strictly commuting
diagram of dg-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{0,-1}\widetilde{P}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{-1,-2}\widetilde{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{0,-1}\widetilde{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-1,-3}\widetilde{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{0,-2}\widetilde{P}_{2}\oplus\Sigma^{-1,-3}\widetilde{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{0,-2}\widetilde{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{0,-3}\widetilde{P}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\binom{e_{1}}{-e_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}e_{2}&e_{1}\end{pmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\binom{e_{1}}{-e_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\binom{0}{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}

(where the negative signs are due to the direction of the homotopy,
going
from

to

e_{2}
).
Thus, the diagonal map is given by the action of

,
which by Remark

represents exactly the homology class
pointed by

in the defining diagram for

.
∎

Construction 7.26

Let us now consider the pushout square
