}$}}\hskip-412.8906pt\hskip 412.8906pt}({C})\right).

In particular, for
F\simeq\mathcal{I}C
, we have

\tau^{B}_{\geqslant\scalebox{.6}{$\bigstar$}}\mathcal{I}C\simeq\mathcal{I}\left(\pi^{B}_{*}\mathcal{I}C[2*]\right)\simeq\mathcal{I}\left(\mathcal{I}\pi^{\text{lvl}}C[2*]\right)\simeq\mathcal{I}^{2}\left(\mathcal{K}^{\text{lvl}}C\right).

Now, we can use the above observation to deduce our initial claim:

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}C&\simeq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{n}\mathcal{I}C^{n}\\
&\simeq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{m,n}\left(\tau^{B}_{\geqslant m}\mathcal{I}C\right)^{n}\\
&\simeq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{m,n}\mathcal{I}^{2}\left(\mathcal{K}^{\text{lvl}}C\right)^{m,n}\\
&\simeq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{m}\mathcal{I}\left(\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{n}\mathcal{I}(\pi^{\text{lvl}}_{*}C[2*])^{n}\right)^{m}\\
&\simeq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{m}\mathcal{I}\left(\dec C\right)^{m}\\
&\simeq\mathcal{H}\dec C.\end{split}

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Given any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that
\pi_{n}C^{\bullet}\in\operatorname{Ch}^{*}({C}^{\heartsuit})

is an ordinary chain complex.
Let

\pi_{-j}\left(\dec^{n}C\right)^{i}\coloneqq E_{n+1}^{(n+1)i+nj,-ni+(-n+1)j}

we want to prove that the above objects define the pages of a spectral
sequence.
We start by defining the differentials of the
E_{1}
page to be

d_{1}^{i,j}\coloneqq\pi_{-j}(\partial^{i}_{C})

Using Proposition
8.10
, we have

\begin{split}\pi_{-j}(\dec C)^{i}&\cong\pi_{-j}\mathcal{H}\left(\pi_{i}^{\text{lvl}}C\right)[2i]\\
&\cong\pi_{-j-2i}\mathcal{H}\left(\pi_{i}^{\text{lvl}}C\right)\\
&\cong\mathrm{H}^{j+2i}\left(\pi_{i}^{\text{lvl}}C\right);\end{split}

the above isomorphism allows us to define

d_{2}^{2i+j,-i}\coloneqq\pi_{-j}(\partial^{i}_{\dec C})

and it’s easy to check that
d_{2}
has the correct bidegree.
As

\pi_{-j}\left(\dec^{n}C\right)^{i}\cong\mathrm{H}^{j+2i}\left(\pi_{i}^{\text{lvl}}\dec^{n-1}C\right)

we can now proceed by induction to show that the
E_{n+1}
page is given by the
cohomology of the
E_{n}
page: using that

\begin{split}\pi_{i}(\dec^{n-1}C)^{j+2i}&\coloneqq E_{n}^{n(j+2i)+(n-1)(-i),(-n+1)(j+2i)+(-n+2)(-i)}\\
&\cong E_{n}^{(n+1)i+nj,-ni+(-n+1)j}\end{split}

we see that

\begin{split}E&\vphantom{x}{}_{n+1}^{(n+1)i+nj,-ni+(-n+1)j}\coloneqq\pi_{-j}\left(\dec^{n}C\right)^{i}\\
&\cong\mathrm{H}^{j+2i}\left(\pi_{i}^{\text{lvl}}\dec^{n-1}C\right)\\
&\cong\frac{\ker\left(\pi_{i}(\dec^{n-1}C)^{j+2i}\to\pi_{i}(\dec^{n-1}C)^{j+2i+1}\right)}{\im\left(\pi_{i}(\dec^{n-1}C)^{j+2i-1}\to\pi_{i}(\dec^{n-1}C)^{j+2i}\right)}\\
&\cong\frac{\ker\left(E_{n}^{(n+1)i+nj,-ni+(-n+1)j}\to E_{n}^{(n+1)i+n(j+1),-ni+(-n+1)(j+1)}\right)}{\im\left(E_{n}^{(n+1)i+n(j-1),-ni+(-n+1)(j-1)}\to E_{n}^{(n+1)i+nj,-ni+(-n+1)j}\right)}\\
&\cong\mathrm{H}^{(n+1)i+nj,-ni+(-n+1)j}E_{n}\end{split}

concluding the existence proof.

The convergence statement follows at once from Remark
9.5
.
∎

10.
Examples

In what follows, we present some results which appeared recently in the
literature using the language developed in the rest of the paper.
The aim of this section is to give a new perspective on known results, hence
we make no claims of originality regarding the results presented; still, we
believe that looking at these results through the lenses of
coherent cochain complexes buys some insight about these results and adds to
the clarity of their statement. In fact, some of the examples discussed here
motivated the investigation of the formalism of coherent chain complexes in
the first place.

Example 10.1
[
Ant19
]

Let
k
be a commutative ring, and let
\mathrm{Sch}^{\natural}_{k}
denote
the category of qcqs
k
-schemes whose cotangent complex
\mathrm{L}_{X/k}

has Tor-amplitude concentrated in
[0,1]
. Then

(1)

there exists a functor
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{C}^{-}(-/k)\colon\mathrm{Sch}^{\natural,\mathrm{op}}_{k}\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{D}(k))

sending each scheme
X
to a coherent cochain complex
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{C}^{-}(X/k)

such that

(a)

the total homology of
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{C}^{-}(X/k)
is given by negative cyclic
homology:

\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{H}\mathbb{C}^{-}(X/k))\simeq\mathrm{HC}^{-}(X/k)

(b)

the components of
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{C}^{-}(X)
are given up to a shift by
truncations of the Hodge-completed derived de Rham complex:

\mathbb{H}\mathbb{C}^{-}(X/k)^{n}\simeq\widehat{\mathrm{L}\Omega}^{\geqslant n}_{X/k}[3n]

(c)

the coherent cochain complex
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{C}^{-}(X/k)
generates a conditionally
convergent spectral sequence

E_{1}^{i,j}\cong H^{j+3i}\left(\widehat{\mathrm{L}\Omega}^{\geqslant i}_{X/k}\right)\Longrightarrow\pi_{-i-j}\mathrm{HC}^{-}(X/k)

(2)

there exists a functor
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}(-/k)\colon\mathrm{Sch}^{\natural,\mathrm{op}}_{k}\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{D}(k))

sending each scheme
X
to a coherent cochain complex
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}(X/k)

such that

(a)

the total homology of
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}(X/k)
is given by periodic homology:

\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}(X/k))\simeq\mathrm{HP}(X/k)

(b)

the components of
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}(X/k)
are given by shifts of
the Hodge-completed derived de Rham complex:

\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}(X/k)^{n}\simeq\widehat{\mathrm{L}\Omega}_{X/k}[3n]

(c)

the coherent cochain complex
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}(X/k)
generates a conditionally
convergent spectral sequence

E_{1}^{i,j}\cong H^{j+3i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X/k)\Longrightarrow\pi_{-i-j}\mathrm{HP}(X/k).

Remark 10.2

Let
k
be an animated ring
18
18

18

following
[
CS19
]
,
an element of the
\infty
-category obtained by Dwyer–Kan localization of the 1-category
of simplicial commutative rings at the subcategory of weak equivalences
;
then, the hull-shelled complex of the Hodge-completed derived de Rham complex

\widehat{\mathrm{L}\Omega}_{X/k}
with the Hodge filtration

\mathbb{L}\Omega_{X/k}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}\left(\widehat{\mathrm{L}\Omega}^{\geqslant\scalebox{.6}{$\bigstar$}}_{X/k}\right)

is such that its components are successive exterior powers of the cotangent
complex:

\left(\mathbb{L}\Omega_{X/k}\right)^{n}\simeq\Lambda^{n}\mathrm{L}_{R/k};

thus, we can represent
\mathbb{L}\Omega_{X/k}
as

\cdots 0\to R\to\mathrm{L}_{R/k}\to\Lambda^{2}\mathrm{L}_{R/k}\to\Lambda^{3}\mathrm{L}_{R/k}\to\cdots

showing how it can be seen as a homotopy coherent
generalization of the usual algebraic de Rham complex.

Example 10.3
[
Rak20
]

With notation as in
10.2
,
\mathbb{L}\Omega_{X/k}

can be given the structure of a derived commutative algebra in coherent
cochain complexes (i.e. a module over a suitably defined

\infty
-operad
\mathrm{LSym}
; see
[
Rak20
, §4]
for details);
the resulting object is initial in the full subcategory of

\mathrm{LMod}_{\mathrm{LSym}}\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Mod}_{k})
consisting of objects
C
that are
concentrated in non-negative degrees and are equipped with a map of
k
-algebras

R\to C^{0}
.
Moreover, the first differential of the complex
\partial^{0}\colon R\to\mathrm{L}_{R/k}
is given by the universal derivation.

Example 10.4
[
BMS19
]
,
[
AN20
]

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p
, and let

\mathrm{Alg}^{\text{ind-sm}}_{k}
denote
the category of ind-smooth
k
-algebras. Then, there exists a functor

\mathbb{T}\mathbb{P}\colon\mathrm{Alg}^{\text{ind-sm}}_{k}\to\operatorname{Ch}^{*}(\mathrm{Sp})
sending a
k
-algebra

R
to a coherent cochain complex
\mathbb{T}\mathbb{P}(R)
such that:

(1)

the total homology of
\mathbb{T}\mathbb{P}(R)
is given by topological periodic
homology:

\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{P}(R))\simeq\mathrm{TP}(R).

(2)

the components of
\mathbb{T}\mathbb{P}(R)
are given by the de Rham–Witt complex

\mathbb{T}\mathbb{P}(R)^{n}\simeq\mathrm{W}\Omega^{\bullet}_{R}[3n];

in particular,
when
R
is smooth, the components are given by crystalline
cohomology

\mathbb{T}\mathbb{P}(R)^{n}\simeq\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\text{crys}}(R/\mathrm{W}(k))[3n];

(3)

the coherent cochain complex
\mathbb{T}\mathbb{P}(R)
generates a conditionally
(strongly, if
\Spec R
has finite dimension over
\Spec k
)
convergent spectral sequence

E_{1}^{i,j}\cong H_{\mathrm{W}\Omega}^{j+3i}(R)\Longrightarrow\pi_{-i-j}\mathrm{TP}(R);

in particular, for
R
smooth, we get

E_{1}^{i,j}\cong H_{\text{crys}}^{j+3i}(R/W(k))\Longrightarrow\pi_{-i-j}\mathrm{TP}(R).

Appendix A
\infty
-categorical and stable nerve-realization paradigm

The content of this appendix is well-known to experts, but to the best of
our knowledge there is no systematic presentation of these results in the
existing literature. Thus we thought it appropriate to include them here for
the convenience of the reader.
We begin by showing a “density formula” for
\infty
-categorical presheaves,
then proceed to describe the nerve-realization paradigm in the unstable and
in the stable case. For the unstable case, we closely follow the
presentation of the analogue results in ordinary category theory given in

[
Lor21
, Chapter 3]
, adapting the few things that need to be adapted in
order to translate the arguments to the
\infty
-categorical setting.
For the stable case, we leverage on results of
[
BGT13
]
and

[
Nik16
]
to give a description of stable nerves.

A.1.
A “density formula” for presheaves

One of the features making the
\infty
-category of presheaves of a small
\infty
-category
{C}
so
important, is the fact that the Yoneda embedding is in some sense the
“free cocompletion functor” (see
[
Lur09
, 5.1.5.6]
for a precise statement
and a proof). In particular, every presheaf is the colimit of representable
ones (see
[
Lur09
, 5.1.5.8]
). The proof contained in
[
Lur09
]
of this last
fact is somewhat indirect, as it does not explicitly give a way to
construct for any presheaf
P
, a diagram having
P
as a colimit. As
later we will need such an explicit diagram, we will now show that, similarly
to what happens in the ordinary case, any presheaf is the colimit of the
inclusion of a suitable comma category in the category of presheaves.

The following is just a special case of the most general definition of a
comma
\infty
-category it is possible to give, but it is general enough for our purposes.

Definition A.1

Let
F\colon{C}\to{D}
be a functor between
\infty
-categories, and
d\in{D}

an object. We will denote by
(F\downarrow d)
the
comma category

obtained as the following pullback

(16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\downarrow d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}_{/d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

Lemma A.2

The map
(F\downarrow d)\to{C}
is a right fibration
classifying
\Map_{{D}}\left(F(-),d\right)
.

Proof.

As the projection
{D}_{/d}\to{D}
classifies
\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{d}
,

[
Lur09
, 3.2.1.4]
implies that the pullback
(
16
) classifies the composite
\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{d}\circ F
.
∎

The above proposition, together with the Yoneda lemma, specialize to
presheaves as follows.

Remark A.3

By Lemma
A.2
, the right fibration

(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P)\to{C}
classifies the functor

\Map_{\mathcal{P}({C})}(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{-},P)
,
but, by
[
Lur09
, 5.5.2.1]
, this is precisely
P
, hence

(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P)\to{C}
is a right fibration classifying
P
.

Proposition A.4

Let
{C}
be a small
\infty
-category, and
P
any presheaf on
{C}
.
Then

P\simeq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits\left((\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P)\xrightarrow{\pi}{C}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}}\mathcal{P}({C})\right).

Proof.

We will prove that
P
and
X
corepresent the same functor in
\mathcal{P}({C})
.
Given any presheaf
Y
, have that

(17)

\Map_{\mathcal{P}({C})}\left(\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{\alpha\in(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P)}\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\circ\pi(\alpha),Y\right)\simeq\lim_{\alpha\in(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P)}\Map_{\mathcal{P}({C})}(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{\pi(\alpha)},Y)

Let us now notice that, by Lemma
A.2
, the functor

\Map_{\mathcal{P}({C})}(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\circ\pi(-),Y)\colon(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P)\to{S}

is classified by the right fibration

(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\circ\pi\downarrow Y)\to(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P)

hence, by
[
Lur09
, 3.3.3.4]
, (
17
)
is equivalent to the space of sections

(18)

\Map_{/(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P)}\left((\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P),(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\circ\pi\downarrow Y)\right).

Let us note that, as
(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\circ\pi\downarrow Y)
fits in the following
pasting of pullback squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\circ\pi\downarrow Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}({C})_{/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}({C})}

we have that (
18
) is equivalent to

\Map_{/{C}}\left((\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P),(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow Y)\right),

and, by Remark
A.3
, and by virtue of the
straightening/unstraightening equivalence, we have that

\begin{split}\Map_{/{C}}\left((\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow P),(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\downarrow Y)\right)&\simeq\Map_{/{C}}\left(\Un(P),\Un(Y)\right)\\
&\simeq\Map_{\mathcal{P}({C})}\left(\St\left(\Un(P)\right),Y\right)\\
&\simeq\Map_{\mathcal{P}({C})}\left(P,Y\right),\end{split}

completing the proof.
∎

A.2.
The unstable nerve-realization paradigm

With the density formula at our disposal, we can now easily adapt the
results on nerves and realizations from ordinary category theory to the
coherent setting. In order to do so, it will be useful to express Kan
extensions using co/ends, hence we will start by recollecting some definitions
and proving a few useful lemmata.

Definition A.5

[
Lur17
, 5.2.1.1]
,
[
GHN17
, 2.2]

Let
{C}
be an
\infty
-category, and let
\varepsilon\colon\reflectbox{
\scalebox{1.5}[1.1]{\text{$\mathbbm{\Delta}$}}}\to\reflectbox{
\scalebox{1.5}[1.1]{\text{$\mathbbm{\Delta}$}}}
be the functor
given by
[n]\mapsto[n]\star[n]^{\mathrm{op}}
.
The
twisted arrow category

\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}
is the simplicial set given
by
\varepsilon^{*}{C}
.

Remark A.6

In particular, we have

(\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C})_{n}\cong\Hom(\Delta^{n}\star(\Delta^{n})^{\mathrm{op}},{C})

and two canonical projections
\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\to{C}
and
\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\to{C}^{\mathrm{op}}

induced by the natural transformations
\Delta^{\bullet}\to\Delta^{\bullet}\star(\Delta^{\bullet})^{\mathrm{op}}
and
(\Delta^{\bullet})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\Delta^{\bullet}\star(\Delta^{\bullet})^{\mathrm{op}}
. By
[
Lur17
, 5.2.1.3]
, the induced map

\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\to{C}\times{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a right fibration, and by

[
Lur17
, 5.2.1.11]
it classifies the bifunctor
\Map_{C}\colon{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}\to{S}
. Equivalently, the opposite map

(\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C})^{\mathrm{op}}\to{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}
is the left fibration classifying
the bifunctor
\Map_{C}
.

Definition A.7

[
GHN17
, 2.5]

If
F\colon{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}\to{D}
is a functor of
\infty
-categories, the

end
(resp.
coend
) of
F
is defined to be the limit
(resp. colimit) of the composite functor

\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\to{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}\to{D}

and is denoted by

\int_{c\in{C}}F\quad\left(\text{ resp. }\quad\int^{c\in{C}}F\right).

Lemma A.8

Both projection maps
\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\to{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\to{C}
are
final and initial functors.

19
19

19

Sometimes in the literature the terms “cofinal functor”
and “final functor” are used in place of what we call respectively “final
functor” and “initial functor”.

Proof.

We will prove that the projection
\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\to{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
is both final and
initial, the other case being entirely analogous.
Let us start by proving finality.
By
[
Lur09
, 4.1.3.1]
, it is enough to check the fibers of

\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\to{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
are weakly contractible (notice that, for any
C\in{C}
, as
{C}_{C/}
has an initial object, it is weakly contractible,
and thus
\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\times_{{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}{C}_{C/}
has the same homotopy type
of
\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\times_{{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}\{C\}
).
Given any
C\in{C}
, let us denote the fiber
\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\times_{{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}\{C\}

by
(\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C})_{C}
.
We claim that each
(\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C})_{C}
is equivalent to
{C}_{C/}
as an
\infty
-category,
and thus weakly contractible.

By pasting of pullbacks, we know that
(\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C})_{C}
fits into a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C})_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}\times{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}

and, again by pasting of pullbacks, we know we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map_{C}(C,D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C})_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}\times{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\times\mathrm{id}}

where both squares are Cartesian. As

\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C}\to{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}
is a right fibration, hence

(\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C})_{C}\to{C}
is a right fibration as well. But, as we identified
the fibers over any
D\in{C}
as
\Map(C,D)
, this has to be the
classifying fibration for
\Map(C,-)
, and thus
(\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C})_{C}
has to be
equivalent to
{C}_{C/}
as desired.
Recall that, by definition, a functor
F\colon{C}\to{D}
is initial if
and only if its opposite functor
F^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{D}^{\mathrm{op}}
is final.
The initiality statement follows from an analogous argument
applied to the opposite projection
(\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C})^{\mathrm{op}}\to{C}
, using
that the map
(\scalebox{.9}{{tw}}{C})^{\mathrm{op}}\to{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}
is a left fibration.
∎

The above lemma has as an immediate consequence that a co/end with a dummy
variable is just a co/limit.

Corollary A.9

Let
F\colon{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}\to{D}
be a functor that factors
through the projection
{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}\to{C}
(resp.

{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}\to{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
), and let
\widetilde{F}\colon{C}\to{D}
(resp.
\widetilde{F}\colon{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{D}
) denote such
factorization. Then

\quad\int^{C}F\simeq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{C}\widetilde{F}\text{ and }\int_{C}F\simeq\lim_{C}\widetilde{F}

\left(\text{ resp. }\int^{C}F\simeq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}\widetilde{F}\text{ and }\int_{C}F\simeq\lim_{{C}^{\mathrm{op}}}\widetilde{F}\right).

Lemma A.10

Let
F\colon{C}\to{D}
be a functor between
\infty
-categories, with
{D}

cocomplete. Then

F\simeq\int^{c\in{C}}\Map_{{C}}(c,-)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits F(c)

where
S\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits X\coloneqq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{S}X
denotes the canonical tensoring in
spaces for
\infty
-categories.

Proof.

The following computation shows that the two objects corepresent the same
functor

\begin{split}\Map_{\Fun({C},{D})}\Bigg(\int^{c\in{C}}&\Map_{{C}}(c,-)\otimes F(c),G\Bigg)\simeq\\
&\simeq\int_{c^{\prime}\in{C}}\Map_{{D}}\left(\int^{c\in{C}}\Map_{{C}}(c,c^{\prime})\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits F(c),G(c^{\prime})\right)\\
&\simeq\int_{c^{\prime}\in{C}}\int_{c\in{C}}\Map_{{D}}\Big(\Map_{{C}}(c,c^{\prime})\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits F(c),G(c^{\prime})\Big)\\
&\simeq\int_{c^{\prime}\in{C}}\int_{c\in{C}}\lim_{\Map_{{C}}(c,c^{\prime})}\Map_{{D}}\left(F(c),G(c^{\prime})\right)\\
&\simeq\int_{c^{\prime}\in{C}}\int_{c\in{C}}\Map_{{S}}\Big(\Map_{{C}}(c,c^{\prime}),\Map_{{D}}\left(F(c),G(c^{\prime})\right)\Big)\\
&\simeq\int_{c^{\prime}\in{C}}\Map_{\mathcal{P}({C})}\Big(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{c^{\prime}},\Map_{{D}}\big(F(-),G(c^{\prime})\big)\Big)\\
&\simeq\int_{c^{\prime}\in{C}}\Map_{{D}}\big(F(c^{\prime}),G(c^{\prime})\big)\\
&\simeq\vphantom{\int_{c^{\prime}\in{C}}}\Map_{\Fun({C},{D})}\left(F,G\right).\end{split}

∎

We now turn to the discussion of Kan extensions in terms of co/ends.

Lemma A.11

Let
{C}
,
{D}
and
{E}
be
\infty
-categories, with
{E}
cocomplete, and
let
G\colon{C}\to{D}
and
F\colon{C}\to{E}
be functors. Then

\Lan_{G}F(d)\simeq\int^{c\in{C}}\Map_{{D}}(Gc,d)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits F(c).

Proof.

Unraveling the definitions (see
[
Lur09
, 4.3.3.2]
and
[
Lur09
, 4.3.2.2]
),
we know that

(19)

\Lan_{G}F(d)\simeq\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits\left((G\downarrow d)\xrightarrow{\pi}{C}\xrightarrow{F}{E}\right).

Now, the result holds from the following computation

\begin{split}\int^{c\in{C}}\Map_{{D}}(Gc,d)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits F(c)&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{prop-dens})}}}{{\simeq}}\int^{c\in{C}}\left(\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{\alpha\in(G\downarrow d)}\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\circ\pi(\alpha)\right)(c)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits F(c)\\
&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{\hphantom{(\ref{prop-dens})}}}}{{\simeq}}\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{\alpha\in(G\downarrow d)}\int^{c\in{C}}\left(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\circ\pi(\alpha)\right)(c)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits F(c)\\
&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{\hphantom{(\ref{prop-dens})}}}}{{\simeq}}\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{\alpha\in(G\downarrow d)}\int^{c\in{C}}\Map_{{C}}\big(c,\pi(\alpha)\big)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits F(c)\\
&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{lemma-tensor-functor})}}}{{\simeq}}\vphantom{\int^{c\in{C}}}\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{\alpha\in(G\downarrow d)}\ F\circ\pi(\alpha)\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{eq-ptwise-formula})}}}{{\simeq}}\vphantom{\int^{c\in{C}}}\Lan_{G}F(d).\end{split}

∎

Remark A.12

A suitable dualization of the above arguments shows that

\Ran_{G}F(d)\simeq\int_{c\in{C}}[\Map_{{D}}(d,Gc),F(c)]

where
[S,X]\coloneqq\lim_{S}X
denotes the canonical cotensoring in spaces
for
\infty
-categories.

Definition A.13

Let
F\colon{C}\to{D}
be a functor from a small
\infty
-category
{C}
to a
cocomplete
\infty
-category
{D}
. The left Kan extension of
F
along the Yoneda
embedding
\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}\colon{C}\to\mathcal{P}({C})
is called the

F
-realization
, denoted

\lvert-\rvert_{F}\colon\mathcal{P}({C})\to{D}.

Proposition A.14

Let
F\colon{C}\to{D}
be a functor from a small
\infty
-category
{C}
to a
cocomplete
\infty
-category
{D}
. Then, the
F
-realization admits a right adjoint,
denoted
\nerve_{F}\colon{D}\to\mathcal{P}({C})
.
Moreover, the value of
\nerve_{F}
on an object

d\in{D}
is given by the presheaf
\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{d}\circ F
, or, more informally

\nerve_{F}(d)\colon c\mapsto\Map_{{D}}(F(c),d).

Proof.

By
[
Lur09
, 5.1.5.5, 5.1.5.6]
, precomposition with

and left Kan
extension along it give mutually inverse functors

\Fun^{L}(\mathcal{P}({C}),{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightleftarrows}}\Fun({C},{D})

hence
\nerve_{F}
has a right adjoint.
The objectwise description follows formally from the following computation

\begin{split}\Map_{{D}}\left(\lvert P\rvert_{F},d\right)&\stackrel{{\vphantom{(}{}^{(\ref{lemma-coend-formula})}}}{{\simeq}}\Map_{{D}}\left(\int^{c\in{C}}\Map_{\mathcal{P}({C})}(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{c},P)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits F(c),d\right)\\
&\simeq\int_{c\in{C}}\Map_{{D}}\Big(P(c)\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits F(c),d\Big)\\
&\simeq\int_{c\in{C}}\Map_{{S}}\Big(P(c),\Map_{{D}}\big(F(c),d\big)\Big)\\
&\simeq\vphantom{\int_{c\in{C}}}\Map_{\mathcal{P}({C})}\Big(P,\Map_{{D}}\big(F(-),d\big)\Big)\end{split}

from which we see that
\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}_{d}\circ F
is right adjoint to
F
-realization.
∎

Definition A.15

In the situation of Proposition
A.14
, we refer to the right
adjoint

\nerve_{F}\colon{D}\to\mathcal{P}({C})

as the
F
-nerve
.

A.3.
The stable nerve-realization paradigm

When, in the situation of Definition
A.13
the
\infty
-category
{D}

is stable and presentable, we can take the nerve-realization paradigm one step
further.

Remark A.16

Let
F\colon{C}\to{D}
be a functor from a small
\infty
-category to a stable
presentable
\infty
-category
{D}
. By
[
Lur17
, 1.4.4.5]
, we have an equivalence

\Fun^{L}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C}),{D})\simeq\Fun^{L}(\mathcal{P}({C}),{D})

given by precomposition with
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\colon\mathcal{P}({C})\to\St(\mathcal{P}({C}))
,
and, equivalently, an equivalence

\Fun^{R}({D},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C}))\simeq\Fun^{R}({D},\mathcal{P}({C}))

given by postcomposition with
\Omega^{\infty}
.
In particular, we get an extension of the nerve-realization adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}({C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lvert-\rvert_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nerve_{F}}

\dashv

to an adjunction

(20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lvert-\rvert_{F}^{\mathrm{st}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nerve_{F}^{\mathrm{st}}}

\dashv

Notice that, again by
[
Lur17
, 1.4.4.5]
, we have that

\lvert-\rvert_{F}\simeq\lvert\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}(-)\rvert_{F}^{\mathrm{st}}\quad\text{and}\quad\nerve_{F}\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\left(\nerve_{F}^{\mathrm{st}}(-)\right).

Definition A.17

We refer to the adjunction (
20
) as the

stable nerve-realization paradigm
.

Putting together what we got this far, we get the following equivalence.

Lemma A.18

Let
{C}
be a small
\infty
-category and
{D}
be a stable, presentable
\infty
-category. Then,
the stable nerve-realization paradigm gives an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\begin{split}\Fun({C},{D})&\to\Fun^{L}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C}),{D})\\
F&\mapsto\lvert-\rvert_{F}^{\mathrm{st}}\end{split}

Proof.

As
{C}
is small, and
{D}
is cocomplete, by
[
Lur09
, 5.1.5.7]
, we
have

\Fun({C},{D})\simeq\Fun^{L}(\mathcal{P}({C}),{D})

and the equivalence is given by
F\mapsto\lvert-\rvert_{F}
.
Moreover, as both
\Fun({C}^{\mathrm{op}},{S})
and
{D}
are presentable and

{D}
is stable, by
[
Lur17
, 1.4.4.5]
, we have a further equivalence

\Fun^{L}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C}),{D})\simeq\Fun^{L}(\mathcal{P}({C}),{D})

as reviewed in Remark
A.16
, completing the proof.
∎

It is also possible to give an explicit description of stable nerves, similar
to the one we gave in the unstable case, using mapping spectra instead of
mapping spaces. To this end, let us first recall the stable Yoneda embedding.

Definition A.19

(See
[
BGT13
, Definiton 2.15]
20
20

20

notice that we use
the opposite capitalization convention of
[
BGT13
]
to distinguish between
mapping spaces and the mapping spectra.
)

Let
{D}
be a stable
\infty
-category. Recall that, by
[
Lur17
, 1.4.2.23]
,
postcomposition with
\Omega^{\infty}
induces an equivalence

\Fun^{\rm{lex}}({D},\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({D}))\simeq\Fun^{\rm{lex}}({D},\mathcal{P}({D})).

We call the
stable Yoneda embedding
the map corresponding to

under the above equivalence and denote it as
\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}^{\raisebox{-3.18884pt}{\scalebox{.7}{\kern-2.3917ptst}}}
.
We denote the adjoint functor
{D}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{D}\to\mathrm{Sp}
as

\map_{{D}}(=,-)

and call it the
mapping spectrum functor
.

Remark A.20

In particular, the functor
\map_{{D}}(=,-)
is such that

\Omega^{\infty}\map_{{D}}(=,-)\simeq\Map_{{D}}(=,-)

and is the unique such functor sending finite colimits in the first
variable to limits and finite limits in the second variable to limits.
As shown in
[
Nik16
, Remark 6.2]
, the functor actually sends

all
(small) colimits in the first variable to limits, and
all

(small) limits in the second variable to limits.

Remark A.21

The functor
\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}^{\raisebox{-3.18884pt}{\scalebox{.7}{\kern-2.3917ptst}}}
is extensively studied in
[
Nik16
]
. In
particular, in
op. cit.
Section 6 it is proven that it is fully
faithful.

We have the following variant of Yoneda’s Lemma for spectral presheaves
of general
\infty
-categories.

Proposition A.22

Let
{C}
be a small
\infty
-category; then

\map_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C})}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}},-)\simeq\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C})}.

Proof.

By
[
Lur17
, 1.4.2.23]
, we know that

(21)

\Fun^{\rm{lex}}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C}),\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C}))\simeq\Fun^{\rm{lex}}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C}),\mathcal{P}({C}))

and that
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C})}
on the left hand side, corresponds to
\Omega^{\infty}

on the right hand side.
Now, by the usual
\infty
-categorical Yoneda lemma (see
[
Lur09
, 5.5.2.1]
)
we have

\Omega^{\infty}\simeq\Map_{\mathcal{P}({C})}(\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}},\Omega^{\infty})\simeq\Map_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C})}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}},-).

But, by Remark
A.20
, we know that the latter
corresponds to

\map_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({C})}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}},-)

under the equivalence (
21
).
∎

We conclude the section with an explicit description of stable nerves.

Proposition A.23

Let
F\colon{C}\to{D}
be a functor from a small
\infty
-category
{C}
to a
stable presentable
\infty
-category
{D}
. Then, the value of the stable
F
-nerve
on an object
d\in{D}
is given by the stable presheaf

\map_{{D}}(F(-),d)
.

Proof.

The functor
\nerve_{F}^{\rm{st}}
is adjoint to a functor

\chi\colon{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{D}\to\mathrm{Sp}
,
which in turn is adjoint to a functor
\xi\colon{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{st}}({D}^{\mathrm{op}})
.
By Remark
A.16
, together with Proposition

A.14
, we have that

\Omega^{\infty}\xi(c)\simeq\Map_{{D}}(Fc,-)\colon{D}\to{S}

and that each
\xi(c)
is left exact (as
\nerve_{F}^{\rm{st}}
is so),
thus, by Remark
A.20
,
\xi(c)\simeq\map_{{D}}(Fc,-)
, from
which it follows that
\chi\simeq\map_{{D}}(F(=),-)
, and thus that

\nerve_{F}^{\rm{st}}(d)\simeq\map_{{D}}(F(-),d)

as requested.
∎

A.4.
The case of pointed
\infty
-categories

We recollect some variations of the results of §
A.3
that apply to pointed
\infty
-categories.
We will not give full proofs for them, as the arguments are entirely analogue
to the ones used in the stable case.

We will denote by

\mathcal{P}_{*}({C})\coloneqq\Fun({C}^{\mathrm{op}},{S}_{*})\simeq\mathcal{P}({C})\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits{S}_{*}

the
\infty
-category of presheaves with values in pointed spaces.

Definition A.24

Let
{C}
be a pointed
\infty
-category. By
[
Lur17
, 4.8.2.12]
, postcomposition
with the functor forgetting the basepoint induces an equivalence

\Fun^{0}{}({C},\mathcal{P}_{*}({C}))\simeq\Fun^{\prime}({C},\mathcal{P}({C}))

where
\Fun^{0}
denotes the
\infty
-category of pointed functors,
and
\Fun^{\prime}
denotes the
\infty
-category of functors carrying the zero objects
of
{C}
to terminal objects of
\mathcal{P}({C})
.
We call the
pointed Yoneda embedding
the map corresponding to

under the above equivalence, and denote it as
\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}^{\raisebox{-3.18884pt}{\scalebox{.7}{\kern-2.3917ptpt}}}
.
We denote the adjoint functor
{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{C}\to{S}_{*}
as

\Map{}_{{C},*}(=,-)

(or simply as
\Map{}_{*}(=,-)
, when there is no risk of confusion)
and call it the
pointed mapping space functor
.

Definition A.25

Let
{C}
be a pointed essentially small
\infty
-category. Then
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{*}({C})

denotes the smallest full subcategory of

\mathcal{P}_{*}({C})
containing the essential image
of
\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}^{\raisebox{-3.18884pt}{\scalebox{.7}{\kern-2.3917ptpt}}}\colon{C}\to\mathcal{P}_{*}({C})
that is closed under finite colimits.

Remark A.26

Let
F\colon{C}\to{D}
be a functor from a small
\infty
-category to a pointed
presentable
\infty
-category
{D}
.
Then, we have an extension of the nerve-realization adjunction

(22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{*}({C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lvert-\rvert_{F}^{\mathrm{pt}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nerve_{F}^{\mathrm{pt}}}

\dashv

such that

\lvert-\rvert_{F}\simeq\lvert(-)_{+}\rvert_{F}^{\mathrm{pt}}\quad\text{and}\quad\nerve_{F}\simeq U\left(\nerve_{F}^{\mathrm{pt}}(-)\right)

where
U
denotes the forgetful functor
{S}_{*}\to{S}
(usually omitted
from notations), and
(-)_{+}
denotes its left adjoint.

Definition A.27

We refer to the adjunction (
22
) as the

pointed nerve-realization paradigm
.

Proposition A.28

Let
{C}
be an essentially small
\infty
-category. For any pointed and finitely
cocomplete
\infty
-category
{D}
, composition with

\scalebox{1.25}[1.25]{\text{\char 135\relax}}^{\raisebox{-3.18884pt}{\scalebox{.7}{\kern-2.3917ptpt}}}\colon{C}\to\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{*}({C})

induces an equivalence

\Fun^{\text{Rex}}(\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{*}({C}),{D})\to\Fun({C},{D}).

If
{D}
is also presentable, we have

\Fun^{\text{L}}(\mathcal{P}_{*}({C}),{D})\simeq\Fun^{\text{Rex}}(\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{*}({C}),{D})\simeq\Fun({C},{D})

and the right-to-left composite is given by

F\mapsto\lvert-\rvert_{F}^{\mathrm{pt}}.

Proposition A.29

Let
F\colon{C}\to{D}
be a functor from a small
\infty
-category
{C}
to a
pointed presentable
\infty
-category
{D}
. Then, the value of the pointed
F
-nerve
on an object
d\in{D}
is given by the pointed presheaf

\Map{}_{*}(F(-),d)
.

Appendix B
An
\mathbb{E}_{1}
presentation of exterior algebras (by Achim Krause)

In this appendix, we produce an explicit
\mathbb{E}_{1}
presentation of exterior algebras over
\mathbb{Z}
. The main result is the following:

Theorem B.1

Let
\Lambda(e_{k})
denote the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra in
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
represented by the dga
which is exterior on a generator in degree
k
, with zero differential.
Then
\Lambda(e_{k})
admits a description as a colimit

\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}}\Lambda^{(n)}\simeq\Lambda(e_{k})

where
\Lambda^{(1)}
is a free
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra on a generator of degree
n
, and each of the maps
\Lambda^{(n-1)}\to\Lambda^{(n)}
is an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Free_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(r_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{(n-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{(n)}}

Here
r_{n}
is an element of degree
nk+n-2
.

The significance of Theorem
B.1
is that it allows us to describe an obstruction theory for
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-maps out of exterior algebras.
Indeed, to find a map
\Lambda(e_{k})\to R
, we need to provide a cycle
e
of degree
k
in
R
,
a nullhomotopy of
e\cdot e
, a nullhomotopy of a certain element of degree
3k+1

(depending on the chosen nullhomotopy of
e\cdot e
), and so on. Having provided the first
i
pieces of this data, i.e. a map
\Lambda^{(i)}\to R
,
the next obstruction, i.e. the image of
r_{i+1}
, is a version of an
i+1
-fold Massey power of
e
.

Theorem
B.1
will follow from the following bigraded version, where the degree of the generator is abstracted away into a second, formal grading.
We work in
\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})=\Fun(\mathbb{Z}^{\delta},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
(where
\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}
denotes the “discrete” category
\mathbb{Z}
, i.e. without nontrivial morphisms). An object is thus simply a list of objects of
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
.
The monoidal structure is Day convolution. Homology groups now have two degrees: If our object is given by
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, we write
H_{k,n}(X)=H_{k}(X_{n})
, and similarly we can shift in both those degrees,
writing
\Sigma^{k,n}
. Note that
\Sigma^{1,0}
is ordinary suspension.
Whenever confusion is imminent, we will refer to the second grading (i.e. the
n
in
(k,n)
above) as “formal” degree.
Similarly, we will call an object of
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
“formally
n
-connective” if it vanishes in formal degrees
<n
, regardless of “topological” connectivity of the individual components
X_{n}
.
Note that the notion of formal connectivity is somewhat simpler than usual topological connectivity: The
n
-connective objects are closed under any limits and colimits, since those are formed pointwise.
(In particular, the notion of formal connectivity is unrelated to t-structures.)

Theorem B.2

Let
\Lambda(e)
denote the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra in
\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
represented by the graded exterior algebra on one generator
e
in bidegree
(0,1)
. Then
\Lambda(e)
admits a description as colimit

\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}}\Lambda^{(n)}\simeq\Lambda(e)

where
\Lambda^{(1)}
is a free
\mathbb{E}_{1}
algebra on a generator of bidegree
(0,1)
, and each of the maps
\Lambda^{(n-1)}\to\Lambda^{(n)}
is an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Free_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(r_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{(n-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{(n)}.}

Here
r_{n}
is an element of bidegree
(n-2,n)
.

The exterior algebra discussed in the theorem has underlying object of the form
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
both given by the complexes
\mathbb{Z}[0]
.
Another way to express this is to say that there is a functor

\gr\mathrm{Ab}\to\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}),

simply given by applying the “discrete complex” functor pointwise, and it is lax symmetric monoidal.
The
\mathbb{E}_{1}
algebra
\Lambda(e)
is precisely the image of the ordinary graded exterior algebra on a degree
1
generator in
\gr\mathrm{Ab}
.

Theorem
B.2
should be regarded as a universal version of
B.1
.
Indeed, the following lemma shows how
B.1
is a special cases of Theorem
B.2
:

Lemma B.3

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a closed symmetric-monoidal
\infty
-category and colimit-preserving symmetric-monoidal functor
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathcal{C}
, and let
L\in\mathcal{C}
be an invertible object.
Then there is a colimit-preserving monoidal functor

\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathcal{C}

taking the graded object
X
to
\bigoplus_{\mathbb{Z}}L^{\otimes n}X_{n}
.

Proof.

An invertible object determines a unique monoidal functor
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathcal{C}
, since
\mathbb{Z}
is the free
\mathbb{E}_{1}
monoid on a single invertible object.
Together with the symmetric-monoidal functor
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathcal{C}
, we obtain a monoidal functor
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathcal{C}
, as the composite

F:\mathbb{Z}\times\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\otimes}\mathcal{C}.

We also have a symmetric-monoidal functor
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})\to\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
, which simply takes
(n,C)
to the graded object which is
C
in degree
n
.
The left Kan extension of
F
along this embedding is the desired functor, as one directly computes from the pointwise formula for Kan extensions.
∎

For example, we have a monoidal functor from
\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
to
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
sending
\mathbb{Z}
in bidegree
(0,1)
to
\mathbb{Z}
in arbitrary degree
n
.
This sends the exterior algebra
\Lambda(z)
to the exterior algebra in
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
on a degree
n
generator, and our
\mathbb{E}_{1}
presentation to an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-presentation in
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
.

The proof of Theorem
B.2
is based on bar-cobar duality.
Specifically, we say that an algebra
A
in
\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
is
formally connected
if the unit map
\mathbb{Z}\to\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
induces an equivalence of the “formal degree”
0
part,
and the algebra
A
vanishes in negative formal degrees.
Analogously, we call a coalgebra
C
formally connected if the counit map
C\to\mathbb{Z}
induces an equivalence in nonpositive formal degrees.
More generally, we call an algebra
A
formally
n
-connective if the cofiber
\widetilde{A}
of
\mathbb{Z}\to A
is formally
n
-connective as an object of
\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
, and analogously for coalgebras.
With this convention, an algebra or coalgebra is formally connected if it is formally
1
-connective.
We write
\mathrm{Alg}^{cn}(\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
for the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Alg}(\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
on all formally connected algebras, and analogously for coalgebras.

Proposition B.4

There is an equivalence

\mathrm{Alg}^{cn}(\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))\to\CoAlg^{cn}(\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))

given by the bar-cobar adjunction.

This is an instance of more general results on Koszul duality, and can for example be extracted out of
[
FG12
, Proposition 4.1.2]
,
where the requisite pronilpotency condition is guaranteed by our graded connected setup.
Since this in particular involves an identification of the usual 2-sided bar construction with the operadic one for
\mathbb{E}_{1}
,
we also give a slightly more elementary argument,
relying on connectivity behaviour of the bar and cobar constructions which we will require anyways.
The overall structure of this argument is similar to the structure of the argument in Section 4 of
loc. cit.

Lemma B.5

Let
X\to Y
be a map of formally
1
-connective objects of
\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
whose fiber is formally
(k+1)
-connective. Then the fiber of

X^{\otimes n}\to Y^{\otimes n}

is formally
(n+k)
-connective

Proof.

The map
X^{i}\otimes Y^{n-i}\to X^{i-1}\otimes Y^{n-i+1}
is obtained from tensoring the map
X\to Y
with the formally
(n-1)
-connective object
X^{i-1}\otimes Y^{n-i}
.
Thus its fiber is formally
(n+k)
-connective, i.e. it induces isomorphisms in formal degrees
\leqslant(n+k)
. Since we can write the map
X^{\otimes n}\to Y^{\otimes n}
as a composite of
n
such maps, the claim follows.
∎

For a coalgebra
C
,
\Cobar(C)
is the limit of a cosimplicial diagram with
C^{\otimes n}
in level
n
. The
\Tot^{n}
-tower for that diagram takes the form

\Cobar(C)\simeq\lim(\ldots\to\Tot^{n}(\Cobar^{\bullet}(C))\to\Tot^{n-1}(\Cobar^{\bullet}(C))\to\ldots)

with fiber of
\Tot^{n}\to\Tot^{n-1}
given by
\Omega^{n}\widetilde{C}^{\otimes n}
. Here
\widetilde{C}
is the fiber of the counit map
C\to\mathbb{Z}
. If
C
is connected, note that
\Omega^{n}\widetilde{C}^{\otimes n}
is formally
n
-connective (since
\Omega
does not change formal degree!), so it follows that
\Cobar(C)\to\Tot^{n}(\Cobar^{\bullet}(C))
is an equivalence in formal degrees
\leqslant n
.

The connectivity of the
\Tot^{n}
-tower has the following two immediate consequences:

Lemma B.6

\Cobar:\CoAlg^{cn}(\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))\to\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
commutes with sifted colimits.

Proof.

\Tot^{n}(\Cobar^{\bullet})
commutes with sifted colimits, since it is a finite limit of terms of the form
C^{\otimes k}
. Since the
\Tot^{n}
-tower stabilizes degreewise, the result follows.
∎

Lemma B.7

For a map of connected coalgebras
C\to D
, the formal connectivity of
C\to D
(i.e. the lowest degree in which
C\to D
is not an equivalence) agrees with the formal connectivity of
\Cobar(C)\to\Cobar(D)
. Dually the formal connectivity of a map of connected algebras agrees with the formal connectivity after
\BBar
. In particular both
\Cobar
and
\BBar
are conservative.

Proof.

Assume
C\to D
is (at least)
k
-connective, with
k\geqslant 1
. Then so is
\widetilde{C}\to\widetilde{D}
, and by Lemma
B.5
, the map
\widetilde{C}^{\otimes n}\to\widetilde{D}^{\otimes n}
is
n+k-1
-connective.
By looking at the total homotopy fiber of the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tot^{n}(\Cobar^{\bullet}(C))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tot^{n}(\Cobar^{\bullet}(D))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tot^{n-1}(\Cobar^{\bullet}(C))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tot^{n-1}(\Cobar^{\bullet}(D)),}

we now see that the fiber of
\Tot^{n}(\Cobar^{\bullet}(C))\to\Tot^{n}(\Cobar^{\bullet}(D))
agrees with the fiber on
\Tot^{1}
in degree
k
. This fiber is simply
\Omega\fib(\widetilde{C}\to\widetilde{D})
. In the limit, we see that

\Omega\fib(\widetilde{C}\to\widetilde{D})_{k}\simeq\fib(\Cobar(C)\to\Cobar(D))_{k}.

This shows inductively that
\Cobar(C)\to\Cobar(D)
has exactly the same formal connectivity as
C\to D
. The argument for
\BBar
proceeds analogously, using the skeletal filtration.
∎

Proof of Proposition
B.4
.

Since formally connected algebras are canonically augmented (the map
A\to\mathbb{Z}
is determined as the inverse equivalence to the unit in degree
0
, and as
0
in all other degrees, both of which are contractible choices), and formally connected coalgebras are canonically coaugmented, we can equivalently state that Bar and Cobar constitute inverse equivalences between

\mathrm{Alg}(\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})^{cn}_{\mathbb{Z}/\kern-1.63885pt/\mathbb{Z}})\to\CoAlg(\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})^{cn}_{\mathbb{Z}/\kern-1.63885pt/\mathbb{Z}}),

where the subscript denotes the “double slice” category of pointed augmented objects, and the formal connectivity assumption is now stated on the pointing or augmentation morphism. Recall that formally connected objects are actually closed under limits and colimits.

In this setting,
[
Lur17
, 5.2.2.19]
guarantees that we at least have an adjunction, with the left adjoint taking an algebra to a coalgebra with underlying object given by the bar construction, and right adjoint taking a coalgebra to an algebra having underlying object given by the cobar construction.

To check that this adjunction is an equivalence, it suffices to check that the unit
A\to\Cobar(\BBar(A))
is an equivalence, and that
\Cobar
is conservative. The latter follows from Lemma
B.7
. For the former, since we can write every algebra
A
as sifted colimit of free ones, and Lemma
B.6
tells us that
\Cobar
commutes with sifted colimits (while
\BBar
is even a left adjoint), it suffices to check that

A\to\Cobar(\BBar(A))

is an equivalence for
A
free.
If
A
is free on a single element of bidegree
(k,n)
, then
\BBar(A)
has homology concentrated in degree
(k+1,n)
(and
(0,0)
). For degree readons, it is thus a square-zero coalgebra. Since both
\Free
and
\BBar
commute with colimits (the latter because it is left adjoint), it follows that
\BBar(\Free(X))=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Sigma^{1,0}X
with square-zero coalgebra structure for
any

X\in\gr_{+}\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
. Finally, for such a square-zero coalgebra, we have that
\Cobar(\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Sigma^{1,0}X)=\bigoplus X^{\otimes n}\simeq\Free(X)
.
∎

In the proof, we have also shown

Lemma B.8

\BBar
and
\Cobar
take the free algebra
\Free_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(\Sigma^{0,n}X)
to the square-zero coalgebra
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Sigma^{1,n}X
and vice-versa.∎

Under this bar-cobar equivalence, the statement of Theorem
B.2
amounts to giving a similar decomposition of
\BBar(\Lambda(e))
. Morally, since
\BBar
takes a free
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra to a square zero coalgebra, and pushouts in coalgebras can be computed underlying, where
\BBar
turns an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-presentation of an algebra into a sort of square-zero cell structure on the resulting coalgebra. In particular, just the underlying homology of
\BBar(A)
should already tell us how many cells we need.

Lemma B.9

Let
C\to D
be a map of connected coalgebras which is an equivalence in formal degrees
\leqslant n
. Assume

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}

is a pullback diagram of coalgebras. Then
F
is a formally
(n+1)
-connective coalgebra, and in formal degree
n+1
the diagram is a pullback (and pushout) square in
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

By an induction on
n
, we may automatically assume the first part of the claim, namely that
F
is zero in formal degrees
\leqslant n
.
From bar-cobar duality, we see that the diagram

(23)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cobar(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cobar(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cobar(D)}

is a pullback diagram of algebras, in particular an underlying pullback diagram.

Since the fiber of the map
\widetilde{C}\to\widetilde{D}
is formally
(n+1)
-connective by assumption, the fiber of the map
\widetilde{C}^{\otimes k}\to\widetilde{D}^{\otimes k}
is formally
n+k
-connective by Lemma
B.5
. As
\widetilde{F}^{\otimes k}
is even formally
(n+1)k=nk+k
-connective, it follows that the total homotopy fiber of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F}^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{C}^{\otimes k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{D}^{\otimes k}}

is formally
n+k
-connective. It follows that the total homotopy fiber of the diagram (
23
) agrees in formal degree
(n+1)
with the total homotopy fiber of the corresponding diagram for
\Tot^{1}(\Cobar(-))
. Since the total fiber of the corresponding diagram for
\Tot^{0}
is simply
0
, we get that the total fiber of (
23
) agrees in formal degree
(n+1)
with
\Sigma^{0,-1}
of the total fiber of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{D}.}

But since (
23
) is a pullback diagram in
\gr\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
, this proves the claim.
∎

Definition B.10

By a
generalized cell structure
on a connected coalgebra
C
we mean a sequence of
C^{(n)}\to C
, equivalences in formal degrees
\leqslant n
, with
C^{(0)}=\mathbb{Z}
, and compatible pushout diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Sigma^{-1,n}X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{(n-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{(n)}.}

We will call
X_{n}
the
complex of
n
-cells
.

Analogously, we define on the algebra side:

Definition B.11

By a
generalized
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-cell structure
on a connected algebra
A
we mean a sequence of
A^{(n)}\to A
, equivalences in formal degrees
\leqslant n
, with
A^{(0)}=\mathbb{Z}
, and compatible pushout diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Free_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(\Sigma^{-1,n}X_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{(n-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{(n)}.}

We will call
X_{n}
the
complex of
n
-cells
.

Theorem B.12

Every connected coalgebra
C
admits a generalized cell structure whose complex of
n
-cells is given by
C_{n}
. Every connected algebra
A
admits a generalized
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-cell structure whose complex of
n
-cells is given by
\Sigma^{-1}\BBar(A)_{n}
.

Proof.

We first discuss the coalgebra case. We let
C^{(n)}
be the formal truncation which is
0
in formal degrees
>n
. Just like t-structure truncation, the corresponding functor
\tau^{f}_{\leqslant n}:\gr_{\geqslant 0}\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})\to\gr_{\geqslant 0}\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
arises from an adjunction between
\gr_{\geqslant 0}\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\gr_{[0,n]}\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
, where the functors are the obvious ones (forgetting degrees
>n
, and extending by
0
). Here we write
\gr_{[0,n]}(\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
for objects concentrated in formal degrees between and including
0
and
n
. Unlike the case of t-structures, these functors are adjoint in both possible ways! Since the functor
\gr_{\geqslant 0}\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})\to\gr_{[0,n]}\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
is strict monoidal, we obtain a lax and an oplax structure on the composite
\tau^{f}_{\leqslant n}
. This gives us a canonical coalgebra structure on
C^{(n)}
. Informally, it is given by the composite

C^{(n)}\to C\to C\otimes C\to C^{(n)}\otimes C^{(n)}.

If we define the coalgebra
F
by the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{(n-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C,}

Lemma
B.9
implies that
F
is concentrated in formal degrees
\geqslant n
, and that in degree
n
the diagram is a pullback in
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
, given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}.}

In particular,
F_{n}=\Sigma^{-1}C_{n}
.
If we compose with the canonical map of coalgebras
\tau^{f}_{\leqslant n}F\to F
(again using the discussion of adjoints and truncation at the beginning of this proof), we thus obtain a diagram of coalgebras

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\geqslant n}^{f}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{(n-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C,}

which is a pullback (and pushout) of objects of
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
in degree
n
. Since pushouts of coalgebras are formed underlying, the pushout vanishes in degrees
>n
, and maps equivalently to
C
in degrees
\leqslant n
, i.e. agrees with
C^{(n)}
. For degree reasons,
\tau_{\leqslant n}^{f}F
is
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Sigma^{-1,n}C_{n}
, and so the claim about coalgebras follows.

For the algebra claim, we simply apply the coalgebra statement to
\BBar(A)
and apply Lemma
B.8
to the resulting cell structure.
∎

Lemma B.13

\BBar(\Lambda(e))
can be described by the graded dg coalgebra
\mathbb{Z}\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots\}
, with zero differential,
x_{i}
in bidegree
(i,i)
and comultiplication given by

\Delta(x_{n})=\sum_{i+j=n}x_{i}\otimes x_{j}.

Proof.

The bigraded homology of
\BBar(\Lambda(e))
is given by the bigraded Tor groups
\Tor^{\Lambda(e)}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
. The standard free resolution over an exterior algebra allows us to compute these to be given by
\mathbb{Z}
in degree
(n,n)
for each
n
. The coalgebra structure can also be computed either directly from the resolution, or by dualizing and thinking about the algebra structure given by composition on
\Ext^{\Lambda(e)}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
(which is polynomial). Since the dual algebra has polynomial homology, it is equivalent to the algebra given by the dga
\mathbb{Z}[x]
with zero differential, from which we get our description by dualizing back.
∎

Proof of Theorem
B.2
.

We simply apply Theorem
B.12
to
\Lambda(e)
, using Lemma
B.13
to see that the complex of
n
-cells in the resulting
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-cell structure is
\mathbb{Z}[n-1]
.
∎

In the resulting cell structure on the coalgebra side, the
n
-skeleton
C^{(n)}
is explicitly represented by the sub-coalgebra of
\mathbb{Z}\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\{x_{1},\ldots\}
.

We can also study the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebras
\Lambda^{(n)}\simeq\Cobar(C^{(n)})
arising as skeleta on the algebra side more closely:

Lemma B.14

For
n=1
, the homology
H_{*}(\Lambda^{(1)})
is of the form
\mathbb{Z}[e]
, with
e
in bidegree
(0,1)
. For
n>1
, the homology
H_{*}(\Lambda^{(n)})
is of the form
\Lambda(e)\otimes\mathbb{Z}[r_{n+1}]
, with
r_{n+1}
of bidegree
(n-1,n+1)
.

Proof.

Since
\Lambda^{(n)}=\Cobar(C^{(n)})
, we can compute its homology groups as derived cotensor product
\Cotor^{C^{(n)}}_{*}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
, or equivalently (since the cotensor product with
\mathbb{Z}
and
\Hom_{C^{(n)}}(\mathbb{Z},-)
agree, they both simply take a comodule to its primitives),
\Ext_{*}^{C^{(n)}}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
. Since everything is finite type and thus dualizable, this agrees with
\Ext(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
over the graded algebra dual to the coalgebra
C^{(n)}
. Since
C^{(n)}
is represented by the graded coalgebra
\mathbb{Z}\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
, its dual agrees with the truncated polynomial algebra
\mathbb{Z}[x]/x^{n+1}
, with
x
now in bidegree
(-1,-1)
. This truncated polynomial algebra admits a
2
-periodic minimal resolution, and from the Yoneda description of the product structure on
\Ext_{*}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
we recover the claimed multiplicative structure.
∎

Since
\Lambda^{(n)}
arises from
\Lambda^{(n-1)}
by a pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Free_{\mathbb{E}_{1}}(\mathbb{Z}[n,n-2])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{(n-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{(n)},}

and the degree
n
-part of
\Lambda^{(n)}
vanishes by Lemma
B.14
, the upper horizontal attaching map needs to be an equivalence in formal degree
n
. Thus, it is given by a choice of generator for the polynomial part of
H_{*}(\Lambda^{(n-1)})
as in
B.14
.

Remark B.15

From the explicit description of
\BBar(\Lambda(e))
in Lemma
B.13
one can actually explicitly describe the skeleta of the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-cell structure on
\Lambda(e)
: The coalgebra skeleta of
\BBar(\Lambda(e))
are represented by the sub-coalgebras
\mathbb{Z}\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
, i.e. the skeleta of the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-cell structure of
\Lambda(e)
are obtained by applying the cobar construction to these coalgebras.
Given an explicit dg coalgebra which is flat over
\mathbb{Z}
, the cobar construction can be described explicitly as reduced total complex of the cosimplicial complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}

One can see explicitly describe this reduced total complex (as a dga) as the free graded associative algebra on
\widetilde{C}[-1]
, with differential given by the sum of the differential on
\widetilde{C}[-1]
and a component coming from the (reduced) comultiplication
\widetilde{C}\to\widetilde{C}\otimes\widetilde{C}
, with signs coming from the shift.
This way, one can describe
\Lambda^{(n)}
explicitly as

\Lambda^{(n)}\simeq\mathbb{Z}\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle,

with differential
\partial e_{k}=\sum_{i+j=k}(-1)^{i-1}e_{i}e_{j}
. Here
\mathbb{Z}\langle-\rangle
denotes a free associative algebra, and
e_{k}
is in bidegree
(n-1,n)
.

In this description, one can take the generator
r_{n}
in
B.14
, and thus also the attaching map for
\Lambda^{(n-1)}\to\Lambda^{(n)}
, to be

r_{n}=\sum_{i+j=n}(-1)^{i-1}e_{i}e_{j}.

For example, we know abstractly from
B.14
that
r_{n}
can be taken as any generator of the kernel of
H_{*}(\Lambda^{(n-1)})\to H_{*}(\Lambda^{(n)})
in formal degree
n
. But the explicit dgas

\mathbb{Z}\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-1}\rangle\to\mathbb{Z}\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\rangle

differ in formal degree
n
only by the single generator
e_{n}
, so the kernel on homology is generated by
\partial e_{n}
, which is the claimed expression.

Using the explicit description from Remark
B.15
, we can also fix the sign in the choice of generator in Lemma
B.14
, and equivalently in the choice of attaching maps in the presentation of
\Lambda(e)
.

The description of
\Lambda(e)
as sequence of cell-attachments along these elements
r_{n}
gives us an obstruction theory for
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-maps out of
\Lambda(e)
. Indeed, an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-map
\Lambda^{(1)}\to R
corresponds precisely to a choice of element of
H_{(0,1)}(R)
. Having fixed an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-map
\Lambda^{(n-1)}\to R
, we obtain a well-defined element of
H_{(n-2,n)}(R)
as the image of
r_{n}
, and we get a further extension to
\Lambda^{(n)}
if and only of this element vanishes (with choices of extensions being in bijection to “nullhomotopies” of a representing cycle, i.e. a torsor over
H_{(n-1,n)}(R)
).

Definition B.16

Given a map
\Lambda^{(n-1)}\to R
sending
e\in H_{0,1}(\Lambda^{(n-1)})
to
a\in R
, we call the image of
r_{n}
in
H_{(n-2,n)}
an
n
-fold Massey power
of
a
, and write
\langle a\rangle^{n}
.

Looking at the explicit dga description in Remark
B.15
, we see that
\langle a\rangle^{n}
indeed is a representative of the
n
-fold Massey product
\langle a,\ldots,a\rangle
. However, it is somewhat more tightly defined: An arbitrary Massey product depends of choices of nullhomotopies for every adjacent product, nullhomotopies for the resulting
3
-fold Massey products, and so on. In the Massey power above, we choose the same homotopy (
e_{2}
) for all the adjacent products
a\cdot a
, a single homotopy for all the resulting
3
-fold Massey products, and so on.

The obstruction theory discussed above can thus be summarized as follows: Having fixed nullhomotopies for all Massey powers
\langle a\rangle^{i}
for
2\leqslant i<n
, we get a well-defined
n
-fold Massey power
\langle a\rangle^{n}
. A map
\Lambda(e)\to R
taking
e
to an element
a
in bidegree
(0,1)
corresponds precisely to a coherent choice of nullhomotopies for all Massey powers of
a
.
